Namami Kalyanasundaram

Part-II

Hamrahi

Namami Kalyanasundaram

Part-II

Hamrahi

Dedication

I offer the fragrant flowers of these reminiscences

unto the lotus feet of my beloved Bábá,

without whose grace this humble author

could not have penned a single line,

Dedication

what to speak of an entire book.

I feel drenched in the shower of His grace

as I dedicate these writings to Him

Hamrahi

Dedication

[Photograph of Shrii Shrii Anandamurtiji seated in a meditative posture.]
Shrii Shrii Anandamurtiji

Hamrahi's Intimate Reflections

It is almost impossible to know fully, intimately, the Ajana Pathik, 'The Unknown Traveller'; for He remains forever shrouded in mystery. He has chosen to play this clever, artful game since time immemorial. To know Him and His ways, one has no alternative but to surrender and be willing to become a part of that mysterious Self.

Anxious and impatient to unravel the secret of the Magician's play, Hamrahi joined the Magician's party. He became an insignificant member of His entourage, and gradually and subtly the secrets of His workings began to filter into his consciousness. Thus it was that the Magician became less mysterious, more ineffably charming. Plunging headlong into this ocean of enchantment, Hamrahi remained forever intoxicated. As he drew closer and closer to this 'Unknown Traveller', His ways no longer remained secret; in fact Hamrahi became an intimate friend. It was so only due to the Master's grace, so generously showered, and for this bounteous grace and special blessing Hamrahi remains forever grateful.

Is it possible, however, to unravel all the secrets of this divine mystery? No, never. In this respect our lot is the same as that of the blind friends who having heard about the elephant, tried to envision it. Some grabbed the tail, and this became the symbol of the elephant; others grabbed its feet and associated it with a pillar, while others associated the elephant with its ears, the only part they were able to touch and feel. Thus all had a fragmentary picture of the elephant and marveled at what they could grasp.

Likewise, we are unable to comprehend in totality this divine mystery. Yet even this limited, piecemeal knowledge is enough to entice us and keep us forever absorbed in this mysterious ocean of enchantment. If just a drop of the divine nectar can transport the devotee into the realm of bliss, what would be one's state if one could drink deeply and fully of the divine ocean!

Hamrahi's Intimate Reflections

By His grace and favour, I remain a drop in His mysterious ocean; I cannot fathom what it would be like to be lost in its entirety! The

truth of the revelation of that mystery in its profundity leaves one deaf and dumb. How can a salt-doll hope to survey the ocean? In doing so it is lost and blends with the waters—a state not preferred by Hamrahi. As he expressed in the Preface to his first volume, he has no desire to lose himself in Baba's Eternal Unmanifested Consciousness. He prefers to be a part and parcel of His manifested drama, His Liilananda. Baba's liilas are his life's breath, his only support, and he remains forever absorbed in them. The sole purpose and goal of his life is to search out these stories of the Lord, and convey them to His devotees. It is his ardent desire that stories of the intimate secrets of the divine Lord may be scattered through every lane and by-lane. There is good sense in his greed, for Hamrahi has understood full well that his store of treasure will increase, rather than decrease through distribution!

The popularity of the first volume of Namami Kalyanasundaram was far beyond our expectations. This sacred blessing, this holy prasad, is a manifestation of His everflowing grace. An English translation has already been published and the Bengali is soon to follow. Delighted and encouraged by the unexpected popularity of the book, Hamrahi and publisher Pranavatmakanandji stepped up their efforts, each in his own way. Dadaji spent days and weeks traveling and meeting with people, all in this search for more and more wonderful stories of Baba's liilas. As the stories poured in, Hamrahi was as quick to put pen to paper. Namami Kalyanasundaram, Volume II, is the result of this interaction between Hamrahi's flowing pen and Dadaji's never-resting footsteps.

Hamrahi's Intimate Reflections

This second volume is now distributed as prasad to all our readers. Credit lies neither in the search nor in the writing. All glory is His who has made this possible. Stories of the Lord have the intoxicating effect of keeping sadhakas absorbed night and day. Their ears can never have enough, can never be satiated by these stories, for they long to listen to them—endlessly. And endless, indeed, are the stories, unending and infinite as the Lord Himself. If there are any limitations, they are those of the writer. In places, at times, these boundaries have been transgressed. For example, in places, when Baba is said to be speaking, the author has supplied appropriate words using his imagination. Though not always Baba's exact words, they are still true. Hamrahi expresses his sincere and profound gratitude to his Master for having granted him this liberty, and hopes that his readers will agree with him without raising any objections.

The author is fully aware of his shortcomings and limitations. Bearing these in mind, he has been ever cautious that the actual facts of the stories are never sacrificed at the altar of readability. In the first volume, the names of the concerned persons were not disclosed in the text of the stories. This time, instead of publishing them by way of footnotes, we have introduced these honoured and fortunate devotees in the context of their concerned episodes. We have also provided a brief biographical introduction. For in the realm of spirituality devotees are as special, as great as their Lord Himself. The fact that the Lord chose them to be the 'actors' in His divine drama bears testimony to their inherent greatness. They are thus forever intertwined with His divine liilas, a part and parcel of His play. These devotees are indeed truly blessed. With profound humility, Hamrahi salutes these great devotees who apart from being the subject of these stories are veritably their source of inspiration.

Hamrahi's Intimate Reflections

Pranavatmakanandji is now no more in charge of Baba's archives. Even so, he has played a pivotal role in the publication of this book. Hamrahi is duty-bound to acknowledge his efforts and express his heartfelt appreciation and gratitude for the same. I am also grateful to Dada Kalyaneshwarandaji, the newly appointed head of Baba's archives, for his unstinting support, which has contributed to the success of this project. Hamrahi's rambling style needed paring and abridging so as to lend it literary refinement. For this Hamrahi is indebted to Respected Acarya Nishantketuji, Acarya Pratapadityaji, Mangalbihariji and Dr. Prabha Verma, for their co-operation and support. To them I express my heart-felt gratitude. I am further indebted to Acarya Pratapadityaji for the expert and knowledgeable advice in the choice of the appropriate Sanskrit verses that appear at the end of each chapter of the original in Hindi.

Acarya Ragunathji, the newly appointed Head of the organization, has always been a source of encouragement and inspiration. With his Foreword, he has lent his resounding voice to the book. I am deeply indebted to him. The designing and printing of the book has been done by our own assistant Kamlesh, and the book bears testimony to his unstinting efforts. Finally and yet of utmost importance are the readers, Hamrahi's well-wishers and admirers, who are the very basis, the raison d'etre of his efforts. For these admirers and well-wishers who have been encouraging him at every step, Hamrahi has no adequate words of appreciation. Hamrahi has a nephew, Sushil by name. He is entirely devoted to Baba. He is always joking and pulling my legs. He says, "Uncle! You have so many fans, so many 'fans' that for the rest of your life you will never feel hot!"

Truly, a writer exists only on account of his readers and admirers. Once more Hamrahi humbly salutes his readers and admirers for their support and encouragement. He is hopeful that this present work, dedicated at the feet of the Master, will be welcomed and enjoyed by ever-eager devotees. To see his readers absorbed and drenched in this ocean of bliss is the only motivation behind Hamrahi's efforts.

Hamrahi's Intimate Reflections

To live so as to please his Beloved Lord and Master in any and every way possible is the only goal and desire of Hamrahi's life. As he humbly surrenders and dedicates this insignificant work at the feet of beloved Baba, Hamrahi finds himself drenched in His divine ocean of bliss and grace. In the past, the Lord had showered His special blessings on Hamrahi; today too He showers him with His grace, and in future for evermore, He will continue to do so. With this unswerving faith and conviction, Hamrahi humbly surrenders his arrogance and conceit, his all, at the lotus feet of Baba.

ACKNOWLEDGEMENT

At Last, the translation of

"NAMAMI KALYANASUNDARAM - PART II"

is ready for offer unto the Lotus feet of the Lord. I hereby acknowledge the splendid work done by the team of translators. They prefer to remain incognito for reasons best known to them. No effort however can remain unnoticed by HIM and they will, I am sure, be bestowed

with the choicest of grace of the Master.

Preface

There is a popular saying which reads as follows: "The Lord is infinite and so are the incidences of His exploits and miracles". A sadhaka meditating on the Supreme Goal ultimately dissolves into His infinite being. The narration of sweet miracles of the Lord as experienced by the devotees overwhelm everybody and more so the devotees. This is quite evident in the rendering of these stories of Bábá's miracles by Hamrahi.

Shravana, Manana and Nidhidhyasana are the three essential elements to accomplish Dhyana. A sadhaka slowly progresses towards Niddhidhyasana (self-surrender) through Shravana and Manana.

Hari-katha is the sweetest expression of the Divine play in physical form. The verbal expression of His name – Bábá – also carries the same sweetness. Once a writer replied, in response to her elder sister's coaxing when he delayed to respond to his daughter's constant calling of his father, that the repeated call of my name by my daughter is so sweet that I want to keep listening the same without responding. Bábá also once mentioned that whenever my children call me as 'Bábá' with dripping sweetness, I derive extreme pleasure by hearing it again and again.

Hamrahi has referred to duality versus non-duality in one of his chapters. Referring to the subject of duality in the context of the Iśt́a, I am reminded of an opportunity I got to speak to Him about the old concept of Tantra where meditating on both Shiva and Parvati was in vogue. To this Bábá responded by saying that He has not approved of this concept. Later, in one of the Mahdcakras He remarked: "I am a singular entity in Mahdcakra".

Preface

Every devotee expresses his love for Him in his individual style. Likewise Tulsi and Kabir have also expressed their love for Him in their own wonts. Surrendering at His lotus feet, Kabir wrote:

Until I exist, you remain oblivious,
With your expression, I cease to be
In the expression of love
Duality can not survive.

It is my sincere prayer that let all enjoy the sweet rendition of divine experiences of the Lord by Hamrahi thanks to His immeasurable grace and I also wish that the readers also become recipients of that divine grace.

Anandanagar 21-02-2002

Preface

My warm wishes. Ac. Raghunath Prasad

The Snake Samskara

In his commentary on Anandasutram, Baba has said, "When a reaction has been performed, but the consequences of the same have not been reaped — in other words, the reactions to the action are still awaited — these reactions in potential form are called samskaras." "At the time of death," He further adds, "certain accumulated samskaras remain associated with the causal mind. In order to give scope for these particular samskaras to ripen, Prakrti brings this bodiless mind in contact with a newly formed body in the human womb, a body which has a similar wave as that of the bodiless mind."

Strange and mysterious is the play of samskaras. Many incidents take place in life, unexpectedly, suddenly, and we call them "accidents." In fact no incident is accidental; behind each and every occurrence there has to be a cause. When the reaction to an action is immediate, we are able to grasp the chain of cause and effect. Suppose one puts one's hand in fire: the hand will get burnt. The cause here is the act of putting the hand in the fire and its consequent burning is the effect. Occasionally we may undergo the consequences of actions performed earlier in our life and are able to associate the cause with its effect. Take the case of an excessively avaricious person, who acquires wealth through embezzlement. With the passage of time he is unable to bear the burden of this illgotten wealth. So his initial greed was the cause of his consequential pitiful state. However, when one undergoes the consequences of an action done in a previous lifetime, then one cannot comprehend its cause and erroneously calls it an "accident" or "destiny," or an "inexplicable event."

Very often, when beset with troubles and difficulties we curse our fate or reproach the Lord for these seemingly undeserved misfortunes. It is only due to our ignorance that we react so, for in reality nothing is causeless. So also there is a connecting link underlying all our close relationships, the link of shared samskaras. The close family relationships formed in our present life, relationships between parents, brothers, sisters and one's children are all due to karmic bonds created

The Snake Samskara

in previous lifetimes. These samskaras get scope for ripening and fulfillment in this present life, through re-association with these past relationships. Similarly, there can be karmic bonds linking humans with animals, but our limited human understanding prevents us from recognising these connections. Here then, is an amazing liila of the Lord, which throws light on what we have just discussed.

This incident took place in Ghatal, a small town in the Midnapur district of Bengal. There is an Ananda Marga school in Ghatal, where the Principal, an LFT (Full time non-sanyasii worker) used to live. Since there was no sanyasii worker posted in the region, this LFT had to shoulder all the responsibilities associated with the smooth running of the school. His name is Shyam Prasad Chakravarty, and he was initiated in September 1984. He lived all by himself in this schoolroom, the walls of which were made out of bare, unplastered and unpainted mud. Everywhere one could see cracks and gaping holes in these weak and crumbling walls, ready to collapse under any pressure.

Early one morning, soon after his posting in Ghatal, Shyam Prasad was about to get out of bed. As he rubbed his half-sleepy eyes he froze in fright at what he saw. A coiled serpent lay peacefully asleep in his bed! The worker being quite knowledgeable about snakes and their species at once recognized this to be a venomous snake. He was terrified. Strangely, as though it had understood the human's predicament, the beast did not attack, but slithered away, slipping away from the mattress. It is a human weakness to retreat in the face of an unknown adversary; one prefers to attack from the rear. Our worker did likewise. Picking up a staff he went after the snake, hoping to give it a deathly blow. The snake however escaped unhurt and withdrew into its hole.

The Snake Samskara

The next day the story repeated itself. This time the worker was cautious, and blocked the snake-hole with fresh mud. On the third day the snake reappeared and this time escaped into another hole. By now our Principal was quite terrified. He made arrangements for a mosquito net, and with his knowledge of anti-venom medicines, he also procured the drug... just in case. But all the precautions were of no use. It seemed like a battle of wits between the human and the beast. The snake and the worker were equally eager, the one to rid himself of the menace, the other to continue to seek human proximity. No matter how hard he tried to block the snake, it somehow found its way onto Shyam Prasad's bed. In fact it chose to become his bedfellow. The worker was now at his wit's end, helpless and demoralised by the terrible predicament that forced him to spend sleepless nights in the company of a poisonous snake! Finally, in desperation he thought of seeking help from some experienced elders of the town. No one had a solution, and some even scoffed at his fears as the workings of a weak mind.

One day the snake left its slough and the worker showed it around. The skeptics were convinced. Even so, none could find a way to help the worker get rid of the beast. Days went by, Shyam Prasad was unable to resist his frightful and undesirable nocturnal companion. Wrought with fear and revulsion, this terrible incomprehensible mystery had become the veritable burden of his life.

Being an LFT, Shyam Prasad had to go regularly to Kolkata, for reporting to Baba. He had made up his mind that on his next visit he would personally inform Baba about his grave problem. At the same time he was also afraid of the senior workers. As per the unwritten code of conduct, no worker was permitted to reveal his personal problems to Baba. In the following reporting session, Shyam Prasad along with other co-workers was personally present before Baba. After they had prostrated Baba inquired about their personal well being. In a chorus came the reply, "Fine, Baba."

"No one has any problems?" Baba asked again.

The Snake Samskara

Shyam Prasad once again joined in the chorus of voices: "No, Baba." But internally he was longing to unburden himself, to reveal all, and let his helpless tongue do the talking.

In spite of his turmoil he remained speechless, the burning issue of his life remained within, swallowed in a bitter gulp. Suddenly Baba asked, "Where is the worker who has come from Ghatal?" Hearing this the much agitated worker rushed forward and fell headlong at Baba's feet, and remained there. Baba said, "Shubham astu," and continued, "Stand up! Stand up!" Shyam Prasad stood up, hands folded. Baba commented, "Your brow is knit with worries. Tell me what is the matter?" Shyam Prasad remained silent. Bound by rules which he dared not transgress, he was unable to grasp this longed for golden opportunity. Even though Baba Himself had asked the pertinent question, Shyam Prasad could not shed his inhibition. It was then that Baba went on to say, "Even though you may not say anything, I know full well that you have been terrorized by an especially venomous snake. Tell me, is it not so?" This time the worker just nodded by way of agreement, and before he could say anything Baba continued, "There is a poisonous snake in the confines of the schoolroom where you stay

in Ghatal, and every day it slinks into your bed. Answer me, is this not the cause of your distress?"

This time he plucked up courage and said, "Yes Baba! Every morning at dawn, I find this snake in my bed. He is bent upon pursuing me at all cost."

The Snake Samskara

In a calm voice, as if to reassure him, Baba replied, "Yes, I know everything. But you don't need to be afraid, at all. Though this snake is highly poisonous, it is utterly harmless for you. It will not hurt you in the least. You can very well sleep soundly without a care." Shyam Prasad's spirits were certainly revived by Baba's assurance, but he was now very keen to unravel this mystery. As fear abated, his curiosity was aroused. It is almost impossible for the human mind to control its propensities. There is no escaping their web-like snare.

The Lord read the devotee's mind and continued to disclose the secret. "In its previous life, this snake was closely associated with you." Shyam Prasad's curiosity knew no bounds. Revealing further Baba said, "Can you recall, you used to have a very close friend, Bankim?" Shyam Prasad startled on hearing Bankim's name, and he listened intently. Disclosing further Baba said, "You were classmates in the village school in Dhangamatti, in Purulia district. You belonged to the same community."

"Yes Baba!" replied the worker anxiously. He could feel his long suppressed, deep-rooted pain surface again.

"Your friendship was so thick and close," Baba continued, "that people used to say that you were two bodies with one life, two minds with one soul."

The Snake Samskara

Shyam Prasad once again exclaimed, "Yes Baba!"

Baba continued to reveal the detailed saga of the serpent's previous life. "Your friend died in unfortunate circumstances, and on account of his bad samskaras, he had to take rebirth in a snake's body." Because of his strong karmic bonds, he has not been able to forget, even in this snake's body, the friend of his previous life. Don't you remember how in his past life he used to call out to you at night with strange and varying calls? Hypnotised by these sounds, you would find yourself being drawn towards him like a remote-controlled robot. You remember this, don't you?"

Baba's astounding revelation stirred up all the memories of his childhood, and Shyam Prasad began to recall every incident in vivid detail. Mentally addressing Baba, he recalled how Bankim was his inseparable friend. Those were the days when Shyam Prasad lived with his elderly paternal uncle, in his home in Dhangamatti, and Bankim happened to be his neighbour. They attended the same village school and were classmates. They always sat together in class and after school would wander about in each other's company. Their attachment was like the bond between identical twins.

Shyam Prasad recalled how his uncle disapproved of their friendship, and certainly did not like the boys being together day and night. As a result they began to see each other in secret. The friends devised their own code signals, and hearing Bankim's cryptic calls, Shyam Prasad would find some excuse to leave the house. Thus he managed to give his uncle the slip. To avoid causing any suspicion, the friends would frequently change their signals. Sometimes Bankim would call out "ess...esss...," sometimes it would be "crow...crow...." and sometimes "chaagal...chaagal..." Like seasoned spies, they had learnt a trick or two.

The Snake Samskara

Shyam Prasad recalled that fateful day when they were both on their way to school. All of a sudden Bankim felt an urge to go home and relieve himself. Bankim left his books with his friend and Shyam Prasad waited outside his friend's house. Time went by and when there was no sign of his friend, Shyam Prasad anxiously rushed into the house.

A shocking sight awaited him, he saw his friend in the throes of death, gasping for breath, fighting for his life. There being no proper medical facility in the village, Bankim's life could not be saved. To this day they were not able to get any medical explanation for Bankim's death, and his sudden passing away came as a great blow to him. Shyam Prasad remained in a state of shock, agitated and lost in grief. He began to hear those very sounds his friend used to make and in his sleep would walk out of the house into the night. He would walk to the spot where they used to meet and spend time together. Hours would go by in this trancelike state. On regaining consciousness he would return home from the field.

This would take place at least twice or thrice a month and continued for several months. His family knew nothing of this, since they would all be fast asleep. It was only when his muddy footprints were discovered in his bed linen that they began to suspect his movements, and finally realized what was going on. This state continued, and it was only after Shyam Prasad left the village to become a worker in the Ananda Marga school that he found relief from this sleep-walking

problem. However, the memories of his childhood friend Bankim and their days together remained forever with him.

The Snake Samskara

Revealing all in this workers' meeting, Baba went on to explain that just as Shyam Prasad had treasured the memories of his childhood friend, so also after his death and in his present life too, his friend had not forgotten him. Continuing to explain further, Baba said that owing to these strong karmic bonds, Shyam Prasad's friend, even in his snake incarnation, longed to remain attached to his past friend. Baba said that undoubtedly the snake was a poisonous one, but for Shyam Prasad he was utterly friendly. His mind was saturated with the sweet sentiments of friendship for Shyam Prasad, and so the snake would never bite him.

Shyam Prasad felt reassured. He was no longer afraid, but it pained him to know that his dear friend was now living in a snake's body. He recalled how after taking initiation into the Marga, he had tried very hard to convince his friend to learn sadhana, but he had evaded the issue. Now he wished he had forced him to do so. Surely if he had taken initiation, then by Baba's Grace he would have been spared the consequences of his bad samskaras. Once again reading his thoughts, Baba went on to reveal that He knew how anxious he was to get his friend initiated, and each time his friend would come up with an excuse.

He said, "You are sad that your friend could not get a human birth. But your wish will be fulfilled. I assure you that after he undergoes the samskaras of this life, he will get a human birth and then he will certainly learn sadhana. There is only one more month remaining for him, after which he will give up this snake body."

So it happened, even as Baba had said. The snake remained there as his bedfellow for another month. After that he disappeared, never to return again. Undoubtedly he had fulfilled his snake-samskaras. In fact, Shyam Prasad's posting in the Ghatal school was contrived only so that he could experience the proximity of his dear friend, even in his snake-life. The story of his posting is equally interesting.

The Snake Samskara

As said earlier, Shyam Prasad was initiated in September 1984, when he was still living in his village. He was a dedicated sadhaka. Some months later he started working in the Ananda Marga school as a Headmaster. Soon after, he decided to become an LFT. After completing his training he was posted in the school in Daspur, in Midnapur district. Normally as per Marga rules, an LFT is never posted in his home district. However due to certain constraints, the senior workers did not wish to post him outside his home district of Midnapur. So they made a pact with the worker. Every LFT, after completing his training, would be sent to Baba for a personal contact. Before Shyam Prasad left for Kolkata to meet with Baba, he had been carefully tutored by the senior dadas, such that if Baba asked him as to which region he came from, he should say 'Purulia' and not 'Midnapur'. But how is it possible to fool Baba? The very first question that Baba asked, at the meeting, was about where he was posted and to which region he belonged. The worker immediately replied that he hailed from Purulia – after all, he had to follow the dadas' instructions!

Baba showed no reaction, but went on to say, "There is a small village in Midnapur district, by the name of Kisma Dirghagram. There is a house in that village with a wood-apple tree next to it. In this house there is a small Shiva temple. As I look into this house, I see an elderly gentleman and his wife worshipping and offering puja in this temple. They are very particular and regular in their worship. You tell me, whose house is this?" The lie was out and Shyam Prasad was dumbstruck. The house that Baba had described in such detail was the ancestral home of his parents.

The Snake Samskara

Overcome with remorse, the worker at once admitted his mistake. Then Baba told him that he was fully aware that he was compelled to lie under instructions from the senior dadas. Baba smiled His enchanting smile, but added with a hint of sarcasm, "These orange-robed 'sanyasii monkeys' think that they can fool me with their clever lies. But I know all their tricks, I am always a step ahead of them, they can never fool me. However, I excuse them this time, as their intentions were good. So although I will be breaking my own rules, I will make an exception in your case, and I will accept their posting for you in Midnapur. Not just in Daspur, I am posting you now at our very headquarters in Ghatal."

In that same meeting with Shyam Prasad, Baba praised him for some of his good works, at the same time he also pointed out his mistakes. Revealing them, He rebuked the worker. He went on to disclose how on a particular occasion he had committed a theft. The worker was taken aback; he could not recall any such incident and flatly denied the same. Thereupon, Baba projected that very incident, like a film, on his mental plate, and made him view it. The worker saw that his uncle had given him ten rupees and had sent him to make some purchases. After making the purchases, he still had some change

remaining with him. His uncle did not ask him for an account and so he did not return the balance amount to him. Days later, he spent this money on himself, an incident which was also projected before his eyes. Finally Baba asked him, "So was this not a theft? If you do not return a thing to its rightful owner, and instead keep it for yourself, then are you not committing a theft? Should you have done this? Were you not going against the principles of Yama and Niyama?" Baba made him acknowledge his mistake, gave him a small punishment for the same and took an assurance from him that henceforth, he would be strict in following Yama and Niyama. Finally, Baba blessed him that he may become an ideal human being.

The Snake Samskara

Baba's revelation of the worker's innermost thoughts and feelings and His final blessing had a profound effect on Shyam Prasad. Tears of joy and ecstasy rolled down his face. Overcome by emotion he said, "Baba! I'm just a simple ordinary person. You have been most gracious to me, You have taken me under your protection. You want that I should work for your mission, but I have no confidence in my ability. I have not studied much and have just a basic education, how will I ever be capable of working for your mission?"

"Why not?" Baba quickly interrupted him. "Why should you think that the work of my mission can be done only by knowledgeable, highly educated and intelligent people? In fact I do not wish to have my work done by such persons, because they are invariably very proud and egoistic. The greater the ego, the greater the absence of God's grace. Indeed, I prefer to take work from the ordinary simple people of society, people like you, who are willing to dedicate themselves. I do not need the so-called intellectual workers. I need sincere and dedicated workers. I will be with you at all times, and every moment I will be guiding and directing you. Never, under any circumstance should you be afraid. Never, not even unconsciously should you allow a feeling of inferiority to cross your mind." From that auspicious moment, inspired and encouraged by Baba's words, Shyam Prasad surrendered his all at his Master's feet.

Just as an experienced jeweller with his trained eye picks and selects the right stones and skillfully crafts them into polished gems, so also, Baba picked and selected each seemingly ordinary worker and with painstaking care and vision, and transformed these hitherto rough stones into precious gems. Such was His liila, in the course of which He revealed a glimpse of His divinity. Hamrahi has no adequate words of praise for His Mahaprabhu, Mahasambhuti Baba. How can he, with his limited potentialities hope to extol His Greatness? Hamrahi confesses that such an endeavour is beyond his scope, and considers himself unfit to undertake the same.

The Snake Samskara

O Gracious Baba! A thousand salutations to You! This foolish Hamrahi bows at Your Blessed Feet and surrenders his all to You. It is through Your boundless grace alone that Hamrahi's crippled pen has been able to take a few faltering steps. For his errors and inaccuracies, for his brashness and arrogance, he humbly seeks Your forgiveness. He remains forever Your insignificant disciple, Your ever-questioning pupil.

---

Jagadguro namas tubhyam shiváya shivadáya ca,
Yogiindránám ca yogiindra gurúnám gtirave namah.
("O Guru of the world, homage to you, Shiva, bestower of auspiciousness (shiva); O lord of the lords of Yoga, homage to you, Guru of gurus.")

The Desire for Liberation brings the Sadguru

"When an intense longing for liberation is aroused in the human mind, then by the very intensity of the desire, the person is drawn towards the Guru, indeed meets with the Sadguru" (Anandasutram).

Who is the perfect master; how does one recognize Him? From time to time, through the ages, various spiritually empowered entities have manifested themselves. They each have their own significance and importance. In their own way, as per their potentialities, they have endeavoured to give moral and spiritual guidance to society. They have been acknowledged and mentioned in ancient mythology and history, and are referred to as 'partial incarnations', anshavatar or khandavatar. However, not all these holy men who have worked for human welfare in different periods of human history can be accepted as the perfect master, the Sadguru. Indeed, the term purnavatar or 'full incarnation' is erroneously used in reference to the Sadguru. The concept of 'incarnation' or 'avatar' is in itself wrong, for this connotes that the Lord resides and rules in a faraway heaven and descends to earth from time to time. In fact, the Lord is always with us, and His abode is the human mind; hence, where is the need for Him to "descend" to earth?

In the past, whenever in the history of the human race society has reeled under the exploitation of the wicked, and the voices of the good and the moral have been suppressed, then the Supreme Consciousness, with the help of the five fundamental factors, takes a human birth. This supreme divine entity, the Sadguru, appears with the resolve to liberate the virtuous from the oppression of the wicked and re-establish the rule of Dharma. He comes, He plays His divine role, performs His liilas and leaves His body as per His own will.

The question arises as to how an ordinary person may recognize the Sadguru. Those who have unflinching faith in their Guru consider Him their divine master, and ascribe to Him the status of a Sadguru. So how does the uninitiated person, with his limited discrimination, recognize the Sadguru? Are there any particular distinguishing characteristics through which the ordinary person can get a vague understanding of this personality? Prior to the discussion of these characteristics, it would be relevant to say something about the three different levels of gurus.

The Desire for Liberation brings the Sadguru

There are three types of gurus. The first, or the lowest category of guru, gives oral advice but does not give initiation. The guru of the middle category initiates his disciples, but after that he remains indifferent to them. The disciple is left to pursue the path on his own. He is left to falter and stumble along the way and has to progress towards his goal by his own efforts and discrimination. The third category of guru is the highest and the best. He indeed is the Sadguru, who after the initiation of his disciples, keeps a watchful eye over them, who at every stage, every moment, continues to guide them. Indeed He is firm in His manner and is a strict disciplinarian, and by His unsparing efforts leads the disciple to his final goal.

Now a few words on the distinguishing characteristics of the Sadguru. In all there are thirteen main characteristics.

  1. Shanta: One who remains unassailed. He is not swayed by sorrow, anxiety or passion.
  2. Danta: One who has full control over all his sensory organs. Actions and their reactions, sufferings and afflictions do not influence him.
  3. Kuliina: A Mahakaula who by His will can raise the dormant kulakundalini of others to its supreme spiritual stance.
  4. Viniita: Utterly humble.
  5. Shuddhavesha: One whose dress is sentient and simple, without any ostentation.
  6. Shuddhacharii: Of right conduct, one who is pure, honest and sincere in his conduct.
  7. Supratishtha: One who is known to live a moderate, restrained and disciplined life.
  8. Shuchidaksha: Well-versed and knowledgeable in yogic philosophy as well as proficient in its practice.
  9. Subuddhiman: Of a pure mind, with a benevolent intellect.
  10. Ashramii: A householder, leading a normal family life.
  11. Dhyanishtha: Fully established in yogic spiritual practices and sadhana.
  12. Tantra-Mantra Visharada: Well versed in the theory and practice of Tantra and Mantra, and skilled in its debates.
  13. Anugraha-Nigraha: One who is gracious and loving towards his disciples but is also a strict disciplinarian.

The Desire for Liberation brings the Sadguru

There is one more distinguishing feature of a Sadguru; He possesses the following six attributes: aeshvarya (lordship); viirya (heroism); yasha (renown), which also includes apayash (infamy); shrii (full of inner Beauty, capable of attraction); jnana (wisdom); and vaeragya (dispassion). The latter does not imply asceticism or renouncing the world. It means one who has full control over all his propensities, and is unaffected by anger, passion and hatred.

The first attribute Aishwarya refers to the eight occult powers. They are: anima, laghima, mahima, iishitva, vashitva, prapti, prakamya and antarayamitva. Even after knowing about these distinctive qualities, is it possible for an ordinary person, with his limited wisdom, to find the Sadguru? Indeed it is not all that simple. In fact it is difficult and confusing to recognise the Guru, through his ordinary, worldly appearance. However, when the Guru chooses to bestow His grace, then the hitherto inaccessible path becomes easily traversable. The truth is that when the keen desire to attain the perfect Master is aroused in the human mind, when the longing for the Master is deep and sincere and the mind is drenched with this devotional sentiment, then the Guru is easily and certainly attained.

Sadguru Shrii Shrii Anandamurtii has clearly stated in Anandasutram, "Desire for liberation brings the Sadguru" (Chapter 3, verse 8). Indeed the easiest and direct way to attain the Sadguru is by arousing in oneself an intense desire for liberation. The account of the particular incident which now follows is one of the many amazing stories of Baba's liilas. This goes back to the very early days of the inception of the Marga, days when the number of disciples was small, each one personally chosen and initiated by Baba Himself. Later on householder acaryas were also created. However, they had not yet been authorized to initiate, of their own accord, anyone who approached them with the request to learn sadhana. The rule was that any person desirous of taking diiksha had to first send his petition to Baba. This request would, in due course, be formally and orally conveyed to Baba. After due consideration, Baba would either accept or refuse the request, or at times ask the petitioner to wait a little longer.

The Desire for Liberation brings the Sadguru

This is the story about two close associates who lived in Jamalpur, Dasharathji and Ramkhilawanji Aulipur. Both of them were keenly attracted to the spiritual way of life and were genuinely in search of a Sadguru. Their extraordinary desire and deep longing was self-evident. They would often meet and confide in each other their desire to take initiation, provided they could meet with the right Master. A saintly person had once revealed to Ramkhilawanji that the Guru he was seeking lived in Jamalpur itself. He did not disclose the Master's name or address but assured Ramkhilawanji that he would not need to go in search for Him, in fact he would meet with Him in that very town. It seemed strange indeed, for in those days very few people had come to know Baba.

It so happened that Dasharathji was fortunate enough to come in contact with the right person, who introduced him to Baba. He was initiated. Right away, Dasharathji was convinced that Baba Himself was the Sadguru. After being personally satisfied, Dasharathji decided to convey the good news to his colleague Ramkhilawanji. Very soon he got the opportunity to meet with his friend. He told Ramkhilawanji that he had found the Sadguru and had also taken diiksha. On hearing this, Ramkhilawanji's long-cherished desire surfaced and he said, "Take me also to meet with the Master."

Dasharathji replied, "No, no, that is out of the question. How can I take you to Him when I am not even allowed to disclose His name! That's the rule. Accordingly, a petition, giving your name, will have to be presented to Him, and only after He gives His consent, will you be allowed to take diiksha." Thereupon Ramkhilawanji requested Dasharathji that his petition should be forwarded immediately.

That night, mentally communicating with this as yet unknown Sadguru, Ramkhilawanji prayed, "Revered Sadguru, I do not know who you are, but with the fullness of my heart, I long to see you, have your holy darshana this very moment, and receive diiksha. If you consider me worthy of this, kindly accept me and grant me shelter at your feet." In the meantime Dasharathji was not able to get a suitable opportunity to present Ramkhilawanji's petition to Baba.

The Desire for Liberation brings the Sadguru

Some days later, he got a chance to accompany Baba on His evening field-walk. Dasharathji thought this was the right time to present his petition, and was about to speak when Baba Himself turned to him and said: "Dasharath! A petitioner, one "Aulipur" by name, has approached me directly with a request for diiksha." Dasharathji startled. There was only one family by that name in all of Jamalpur. One by one Dasharathji recalled the names of the family members to Baba, but He kept saying, "No, not this one."

Dasharathji did not see the need to mention Ramkhilawanji's name, for he was sure there was no possibility of him having made

direct contact with Baba. After all, Ramkhilawanji himself had requested Dasharathji to present his petition to Baba. At last, when he had exhausted all the names, Dasharathji accepted defeat and uttered Ramkhilawanji's name.

At once Baba interrupted and said, "Yes! This very Ramkhilawanji has approached me directly. Tell him that he has been granted the permission to take diiksha. He may contact an authorised acarya and take diiksha from him."

The Desire for Liberation brings the Sadguru

Dasharathji was quite confused and could not believe what he had heard. How was it possible, he wondered, for Ramkhilawanji to contact Baba directly? To clarify his doubts he headed straight for Ramkhilawanji's residence, and on meeting him asked, "So, after all, you have contacted the Guru directly for diiksha?"

"What contact are you talking of?" Ramkhilawanji interjected. With much annoyance Dasharathji repeated the question, to which Ramkhilawanji replied, "When you have not disclosed the name and address of the Guru, the possibility of my contacting Him does not arise!"

"Are you sure?" Dasharathji asked again.

"Hundred percent sure," Ramkhilawanji unhesitatingly replied.

The Desire for Liberation brings the Sadguru

Thoroughly confused and amazed, Dasharathji went on to say, "Then how did Gurudeva tell me that Ramkhilawanji had directly approached Him with a request for diiksha, and that He has already given you His consent?"

Ramkhilawanji was extremely happy to know that he had received His consent. But the two dear friends were unable to grasp the mystery of this "direct contact" which Baba had disclosed. They pondered over this for a long while. Finally, Ramkhilawanji seemed to recall, and said, "Yes! The day you came and told me about receiving diiksha and talked about the Master, I was very perturbed. That same night I made this fervent and emotional prayer – I said, 'O divine master, I do not know who you are, but I seek your grace. Grant me diiksha and accept me as your own.'"

Even as he heard this, Dasharathji realized and understood that this was Ramkhilawanji's "direct contact." It dawned on him that for He who is all-pervading, there are no mental and physical boundaries. Indeed, it is true that when the devotee experiences a deep and intense longing, when desire reaches its zenith, then the Guru Himself becomes restless. When the devotee longs with single-minded devotion to meet his Lord, then the Lord too becomes impatient to meet with His devotee. The truth is that if we take one step towards Him, He takes four steps towards us. Such indeed is His impatience!

Sáshtháńgaprańatih stutir bahuvidhá, hy etat samastam mayá,
Samkalpena samarpitam tava vibho, pújá grhána prabho.
("Full prostration, varieties of hymns of praise – all of this I offered you with firm resolve, my Master. Please accept my worship, O Lord.")

Salvation for Self, Service to the World

At its very inception, when Baba founded the Ananda Marga organization, He also declared its aim and purpose. This is "Átmamokshártham jagaddhitáya cha." It called for one's personal diligent efforts for self-liberation coupled with selfless service to society. It implied devotion and service, the human being's love of God expressed through serving His creation.

To translate this noble ideal into practicality, Baba created several unique platforms from which He launched His multifarious welfare schemes. He created an independent department, ERAWS, with the sole purpose of coordinating these various schemes. ERAWS stands for Education, Relief and Welfare Section, and each sub-department was entrusted with educational activities, relief work and social welfare projects.

Baba made radical changes in the traditional system of education and gave detailed guidelines for reforms in this field, which included school and college education, women's education and adult education programs. In fact, He infused bold and lofty ideals and revamped the entire curriculum, giving a clear direction to all the educational institutions by founding the Gurukula. As a result, there are today innumerable schools established both in India and abroad which follow this distinctive syllabus and educational pattern. The Chancellor of the Gurukula is the Head of all these activities in the field of education.

Likewise, Baba created an independent organization, AMURT (Ananda Marga Universal Relief Team), especially to organize and implement disaster relief work. AMURT's main activity is to rescue and help those afflicted by natural disasters. This team has rendered commendable service during times of natural calamities, both at home and abroad, and has won many accolades for its humanitarian work.

Salvation for Self, Service to the World

In the field of welfare activities, Baba established several departments that would address particular social needs. These include the Women's Welfare Department, Department for the Visually Impaired, Children's Homes and Old Age Homes. Welfare work was commenced in each of these areas and many projects completed. Good work continues to be done even today. This was not all: special welfare schemes were introduced to cover all aspects of creation, animal and plant life too. This wonderful sentiment has been elaborated and lucidly expounded in His book Neo-Humanism.

In short, Baba gave equal importance to both sadhana and service, as according to Him, they are complementary and mutually dependent. In fact, Baba spent the greater part of His life in introducing and organizing the varied social welfare programs. From time to time, He would personally visit the centers where work was being carried out by His disciples, and inspect and survey its progress. Every project, successfully completed for the welfare of society, was considered by Him as the measure of the disciple's faith and devotion towards the Guru.

The ideal of service that He stressed was made applicable both to the collective body of the organization as well as to individual life. Even the most rigorous and intense sadhana would bear no result if it was not matched by selfless social service. Sadhana is essentially a self-centered effort, and its fruits beneficial or otherwise experienced by the self alone. In contrast, service has a benevolent spiritual value. Of what worth is that sadhana, diligent and intense though it may be, if it does not enable one to transcend the self? Are we not duty-bound to repay our debt to society? Society is in turmoil, suffering and distress is all around us; under such circumstances, would it be right and proper for one to lose oneself in sadhana and remain indifferent to the world? This is not acceptable any more. Such self-seeking and indifferent behaviour can be expected of animals, but if humans too behave so, then 'humanity' itself is lost.

Salvation for Self, Service to the World

In this world, whatever service we receive from society, we needs must give back in full measure. This is the only way to exhaust one's samskaras, or else one will forever remain within the cycle of birth and rebirth. Moreover, the accumulated debt to society will have to be repaid with added interest. This is why it has been said that service to the world and Tantra-sadhana are complementary, both being equally important in the realm of spirituality. Those spiritual masters who do not lay stress on this very important part of spiritual practice are in fact doing a grave injustice to society. For this lapse of theirs, they will have to pay a heavy price. Such is the irrevocable law of Prakrti.

Baba has said, "Human life is an ideological flow." One must have an ideal in life. One who does not follow this two-fold ideal of sadhana

and service, but pursues only the singular path for one's own upliftment, is not a human being, in the true sense of the word.

Human existence is trifarious, physical, mental and spiritual. Undoubtedly the spiritual realm is the most vital for human progress and expansion. Even so, one cannot afford to ignore the physical world. The sadhana that Baba introduced for the benefit of the general public has been aptly termed "Brahma Sadhana," not "Parama Purusa Sadhana" or "Shiva Sadhana." Brahma is the composite Entity, combining both Parama Purusa and Parama Prakrti, the Truth that encompasses both Shiva and Shakti. Within the realm of Brahma Sadhana there exists the union of Parama Prakrti, and this play of Prakrti, its binding Cosmic Dance, results in the creation of this world. The only way one can live in this world and yet be free from the bondage of Prakrti is through the practice of madhu vidya. Ignoring or being indifferent to the created world will not help at all. This then is the supreme distinguishing feature of the Brahma Sadhana taught by Shrii Shrii Anandamurtiji.

Salvation for Self, Service to the World

Before Baba's advent, the true import and purpose of samnyasa was lost to society. Samnyasa meant renouncing the world and withdrawing into the jungles and caves for the self-centered pursuit of liberation. According to Baba, this did not lead to self-liberation; rather He derisively called it a practice in self-deception. Shrii Shankaracarya in his mayavada philosophy has aphoristically referred to the world as illusion: "Brahma satyam, jagata mithya." Baba never supported this view. In Anandasutram Baba has given a new, more profound and far more acceptable definition, wherein He says, "Brahma satyam, jagad api satyam apekshikam" (Chapter 2, Verse 14). This quinquelemental world may be a passing and an everchangingshow, but it is certainly not an illusion, and certainly not false. Such a philosophical explanation was totally unacceptable. If the world is illusory and false, then why do we avail ourselves of its many services? According to Baba, the world is not an illusion: it is a relative truth. In our search for Truth, for the Supreme Brahma, we cannot ignore this world of relative truth.

One of the first principles Baba propounded was that of "Subjective Approach through Objective Adjustment." He laid great stress on this, asserting that human beings should move towards the Supreme Entity while maintaining a proper balance with this physical world. Brahma Sadhana is a must for every human being, but one has to pursue this path while maintaining a proper adjustment with this manifested world.

Salvation for Self, Service to the World

As for the Sannyasiis who roam the forests and live in the Himalayan caves, do they not partake of the bounties of this world? The air they breathe, the water they drink, the fruits they pluck from the trees, are these not the gifts of this quinquelemental world? Living in remote caves and jungles, they are nevertheless subject to hunger and readily pluck the fruits of the forests. However, they will not make the effort to plant more fruit-bearing trees for the benefit of the future generations! Do they have no duties and obligations towards the human race? Each and every aspect of social welfare has its importance and relevance and that is why Baba laid stress on social service, making it an integral part of Tantra Sadhana. In fact He re-established the role of service, which must play a prominent part in one's spiritual endeavours.

The story of Baba's liila that follows illustrates practically the aforesaid ideological discussion. In those days, when Baba resided in Jamalpur, it was His regular routine to take an evening stroll to the tiger's grave. At times some strange and mysterious sadhakas would also come to meet with Him. On quite a few occasions Margii brothers accompanying Baba on these evening walks had witnessed these strange meetings. Apparently, these strange sadhakas were closely attached to Baba, but they were not known to anyone in the Marga. Margiis had already heard the stories about Kalikananda and other such evolved sadhakas, so these incidents further fuelled their curiosity. When some strange and unknown sadhaka would occasionally come to meet Baba at the grave, He invariably asked the Margii disciples to leave so that He could talk with the stranger in private. This naturally made the Margiis more curious to know what was being discussed. With each new experience their curiosity increased, but they were unable to unravel the secret, remaining deeply puzzled. Finally, one day an incident occured which threw light on the kind of relationship these sadhakas had with Baba.

Salvation for Self, Service to the World

One day, three of Baba's disciples, Acarya Naginaji, Acarya Vindhyachal Pandeyji and Lalanji happened to accompany Baba on His evening walk. Every day Baba would pass by a small culvert to reach the field beyond. That day they came upon a stranger standing by the culvert. His white-as-cotton tangled hair and beard, and his torn and tattered clothing added to his strange and ragged look. As he approached Baba he raised his hands upwards and in a tormented

voice cried out, "Jai Shiva Shankar! Oh Lord, many days have passed in anxious waiting!"

The three disciples were puzzled by these words. For whom had this stranger been waiting and why? Without a word, Baba signaled to the stranger to follow them, and then continued to talk with His disciples, good-humouredly, pleasantly chatting all the way to the tiger's grave. On reaching the grave Baba's mood suddenly changed, and with all seriousness He said, "Look, you all are constantly around me night and day. This poor man has been wandering around for many days and has not got an opportunity to speak with me. Perhaps he has something urgent to discuss, which is why he seems so impatient. Do one thing, today you all return home, as I have to talk to him. As your time will be unnecessarily wasted, it would be better if you went back now."

Salvation for Self, Service to the World

The three disciples remained rooted, staring in disbelief. They could not help feeling upset with Baba's seemingly harsh and severe tone. Most of all, they did not want to leave. But how could they protest! Helplessly they turned back and began to walk towards the city. They had barely walked for a short distance when a similar thought crept into each of their minds. They all thought that they should hide themselves in the surrounding area and observe the 'goings-on', and thus try to get to the bottom of this mystery. They would wait till their discussion was over and Baba would set off for His residence. Then they would surround this new-comer and get the inside story from him. So they thought of a plan to cut off all the roads that led from the grave, posting themselves at each of the three exit points. They were sure that the stranger would have to leave by any one of these three routes and in doing so, he would be spotted by one of them. So each one of them took up their positions at their chosen spot and waited anxiously. They were quite sure that that night they would be able to satisfy their long accumulated curiosities and the secret would finally be out.

It was around eight o'clock in the night and usually Baba would sit at the grave till ten. But that day the hours ticked away, well past ten, and it was soon eleven thirty in the night. The three disciples were tired and restless, and were fast losing their patience. Finally one of them decided to walk towards the grave. As he began to approach the grave, he heard not a sound, and as he reached the grave he realized that there was no one there. Both the stranger and Baba had disappeared!

He retraced his steps and met up with the other two disciples. They were all astonished and confused that none of them had been able to spot Baba or the stranger returning from the grave. How could Baba and the stranger have given them the slip? Acutely disappointed and thoroughly baffled they had no option but to return home.

Fortunately, just two days later, Naginaji got another opportunity to accompany Baba on His walk. Perhaps Baba too had been waiting for this opportunity. With seeming innocence, Baba turned to Naginaji and asked,

Salvation for Self, Service to the World

"Nagina! That day the three of you had drawn up an elaborate plan to meet up with the stranger. So, did you get to meet with him? Were you able to talk to him? Could you get to know any secrets? Were you able to fathom the mystery?"

Knowing full well Baba's antaryamitva powers, Naginaji did not try to hide anything. He related to Baba every detail of their plan, and told Him how the three of them had gone home that night with utter disappointment. Naginaji said to Baba, "We waited and kept watch till midnight, having posted ourselves at all the routes. But we don't know how you both vanished from the scene!"

Baba smiled. A while later He said, "You know Nagina, that night there were just three of you. However, even if there had been three hundred of you, all on horseback, you would still not have succeeded in locating that mysterious person. So elevated a soul was he." On hearing these words, Naginaji's curiosity knew no bounds. He eagerly continued to question Baba on the identity of the stranger. Baba was unable to stall His disciple's persistent queries and finally He had to reveal all.

Baba told Nagina that the old man was an advanced sadhaka who had been diligently practicing sadhana for many long years. He was a resident of Viratnagar in Nepal. Baba continued to say, "His spiritual sadhana is almost completed and he now wishes to leave his physical body. He needs to take prior permission before giving up his body, and that is why he has been roaming the streets of Jamalpur since some days now."

Salvation for Self, Service to the World

"Why does he need 'prior permission' Baba?" Naginaji interrupted with child-like eagerness. Baba went on to explain, that such is the rule that when the Sadguru is present on earth in His physical form, then those sadhakas who are desirous of death and final release from rebirth must seek His prior permission before giving up their physical body.

"So did you give him your permission?" Naginaji asked with ever increasing curiosity.

"No, I have not given him my permission. He has been given a duty to perform, and only after he completes the assigned duty will he be allowed to give up his body." Said Baba.

"What duty Baba?" There seemed no end to Naginaji's questions.

Salvation for Self, Service to the World

Thereupon Baba explained at length, saying, "I have now made a new rule. Accordingly, every sadhaka, even great ones who may have spent all their lives in sadhana, cannot attain final release unless they have rendered service to society. It has now been made compulsory for all to do social service, and thus return the debt one owes to society. The sadhaka who met me the day before yesterday had certainly completed his sadhana, but he had not fulfilled the essential condition of social service. Without serving His creation, one has no right to seek the grace of that supremely gracious entity."

"So he was forced to return empty-handed!" Naginaji asked.

Baba continued, "I told the sadhaka that before he can attain the final glorious death, he would have to perform rigorous social service for three months. Three months seemed a very long period indeed and the sadhaka was greatly distressed by my command."

Baba went on to explain, "When all one's accumulated samskaras are exhausted through the penance of rigorous sadhana, then the sadhaka finds it very painful to maintain his physical body. In normal death, one is still attached to the body, but in mahamrtyu ("great death") the sadhaka is eager to give up his body. There lies the difference between normal death and mahamrtyu. The sadhaka tearfully entreated me, saying "Baba, I do not wish to remain in this body any longer."

Salvation for Self, Service to the World

"I said I would not accept this."

Then Baba related how the sadhaka continued to plead with Him, fervently seeking His mercy. Thereupon Baba reduced the time span from three months to one month. Even this was not agreeable to the old sadhaka who continued to plead more fervently. So Baba was forced to 'haggle' with the sadhaka, fifteen days, ten days, five days... Thereupon the sadhaka in an agonized voice appealed to Baba, saying,

"Baba my body cannot even wait for five days to attain mahamrtyu. Lord have mercy on me!"

Then Baba related to Naginaji saying "Finally I had to make further concession. I asked him to do social service for only two days, after which he was permitted to leave his body."

Salvation for Self, Service to the World

Then Naginaji asked with added curiosity, "Where is he now and what service is he performing, Baba?"

"This I shall not reveal." So saying Baba brought the conversation to an end. The next day, while it was still dawn, word spread in Jamalpur that the body of an unknown, old sadhu had been found near the tunnel that ran between Jamalpur and Bhagalpur. By now several sadhakas in Jamalpur had heard Naginaji's story about the mysterious sadhaka. One of them, Acarya Haragovindji, while accompanying Baba on His evening walk that day, asked Him,

"Baba, that body that was found in the tunnel, was it that of the sadhu from Viratnagar?"

Baba replied, "Yes, he was that very sadhaka from Viratnagar. Having fulfilled all his duties and obligations in this life, he has now merged with the Supreme Consciousness."

Salvation for Self, Service to the World

Unless one fulfills one's duties and responsibilities towards the created world, one cannot hope to attain the supreme Truth. Like the mythical Flower of Heaven, such a hope would only remain an unfulfilled dream. This then is the irrefutable law for ages to come.

---

Karacarannkrtam vá káyajam karmajam va,
Shravananayanajam vá mánasam váparádham.
Vihitavihitam vá sarvam etad kshamasva,
Jaya jaya karunábdhe shriimahádeva shambho.
("Whether performed by my hands or feet, by my body or my actions, by my ears, eyes, or mind, or as commands carried out, may you forgive all the various offences I have committed. O ocean of compassion, O Shambhu, O glorious Shiva Mahadeva, be victorious, be victorious!")

“Práner Spandana Yuga Vivartana”

Baba has left many wondrous gifts for the benefit of humankind, each a great blessing, and Prabhat Samgiita is certainly amongst His most precious gifts to us. From 14th September 1982 to 20th October 1990, Baba composed over five thousand songs. A profound devotional sentiment permeates these verses, all of which speak of divine love and give expression to man's eternal longing for his creator. In his quest for the divine, in this dialogue between man and his maker, the devotee is at a loss to express the sweet and tender feelings of absolute love and reverential surrender. Baba gave beautiful expression to those tender, delicate sentiments. Prabhat Samgiita is primarily a unique expression of the mute devotee's most intimate yearnings. In one of the songs, Baba refers to the Lord's capacity for antaryamitva, his universality, his all-pervasive and eternal self. The expression is succinct, charming and appealing. The song is as follows:

Ma'yá mukure
Ke kii bha've kii kare
Sabai jána ogo devatá.
Práner spandan yuga vivartan
Sabai shonn gopnn kathá.
Tomhre lukáye kii knrite pári
Tomáre bhula'ye kii bhávite pári
Sakal vika'sh máje
Tumi a'cho snb káje
Niirava madhuratá.

(Its meaning: Oh my Lord, You have a magical mirror with the help of which You are able to know everything. You are able to know what one is thinking and doing. You are able to hear the heart beat of every individual. You know all about this Universe, all the transformation and changes wrought through the ages. You hear every secret thought, indeed I cannot hide anything from You, my Lord! I can never fool You and so I cannot think anything secretly. You are forever linked with this manifested world. You are with us in all our actions but You hide Yourself, remaining forever sweetly silent.)

“Práner Spandana Yuga Vivartana”

These qualities of the Lord, his universality, omniscience and all-pervasiveness, can be a source of help and also a hindrance to a devotee. The advantage is that the devotee can reverentially surrender his all to his Beloved, and leaving all in His hands, he can lead a life free from cares and anxieties. The disadvantage lies in the fact that there is no possibility for the sadhaka to act in secret; indeed, he cannot entertain even a thought without His knowing! Even a passing thought is at once reflected in that maya mukur, that magical mirror. In the ultimate scheme of things, this disadvantage is a blessing indeed, for the devotee is rendered incapable of performing any wrong deed or entertaining any wrong thought, without His knowledge.

Every moment, all the time, the Lord's eyes are watching over all His creation. This wonderful inspiring sentiment is not just enshrined in the words of the song. In fact, during his lifetime, Baba performed countless liilas that revealed His omniscience. By doing so He ensured that the devotee was fully convinced of the Lord's all-knowing, all-pervasive capacity, and that this fact was indelibly printed on his mental plate. By His amazing liilas, Baba brought home to the devotee that the Lord was aware of even the slightest vibrating quiver of his breath, for his "magical mirror" is capable of reflecting every sympathetic vibration of the devotee's mental state. One particular liila-story comes to mind and is related below.

In the Dumka district of Bihar, there lived a devoted disciple of Baba. He was a high-ranking police officer, holding the post of Deputy Superintendent of Police. Within the hierarchy of the Marga too, he was a respected senior householder Acarya. One day, all of a sudden, word got around in the Office that his immediate senior officer, the Chief Superintendant of Police, was suffering from a grave illness that was likely to prove fatal. Occasionally it so happens, that even an otherwise good and upright person can entertain a wrong thought when swayed by greed and envy. This happened too with the Deputy Police Superintendent.

“Práner Spandana Yuga Vivartana”

Suddenly a thought crept into his mind – that if this news was true, and God forbid, if the Chief Officer were to pass away, then there was a sure likelihood of his being promoted. For a fraction of a moment, the police officer was caught in the snare of avidya maya. But

he at once became conscious, and checked himself, thinking, "Oh no! It was totally improper to have had such a thought!" So he forthwith banished that foul thought which had entered his mind for a fraction of a second. Luckily this was a fleeting thought and he was able to erase it completely from his mental plate.

Four to five months later he got the opportunity to attend Baba's darshana in Jamalpur. That day Baba chose to speak about some aspects of human behaviour and conduct that are beneficial in everyday life. The subject of His discourse was, "Paropakar: Punyaya, Papaya Parapiidanam." Baba went on to say that it should be every sadhaka's dharma to be ever prepared to serve humanity, one must always work diligently to serve His Creation. In fact, one should always think about lightening the burden of others, and be ever ready to take on their troubles. All the time one should be thinking as to how one can do good to others even at the cost of inconveniencing oneself. "Sarve bhavantu sukhina sarve santu niramayah": a sadhaka should bear in mind this lofty sentiment.

Baba continued to say that whenever one gets an opportunity to serve someone, one should look upon it as a special Grace and Blessing from the Lord. A good human being is one who works for the welfare of others even at the cost of sacrificing his own ease and comfort, whereas a wicked person does not hesitate to harm others in order to further his own selfish interests. Baba said that one should never think of causing harm to others; such a mean thought should never enter one's mind. It is indeed sinful to entertain such a thought.

“Práner Spandana Yuga Vivartana”

In the middle of His speech, Baba paused and asked the sadhakas, "Is it proper to entertain such a thought?"

"No Baba!" came the immediate collective response.

Then Baba went on to ask, "Suppose a government officer were to think that if his senior officer were to die then he would be promoted to his position. Now would it be proper for him to think thus?"

"No Baba!" replied the sadhakas in one voice. Baba paused a while and then turned directly to the police officer and asked him, "Tell me! Would this be proper?" The D.S.P. was stunned to hear this question from Baba; for a moment he thought he had been struck by a thunderbolt. Suddenly as he began to recall the now long-forgotten incident, he realized with dismay that he himself had once entertained for a fleeting moment this very mean thought.

“Práner Spandana Yuga Vivartana”

He was amazed that after a lapse of so many months, when he himself had forgotten all about it, Baba should now refer to it. Not only that, in front of all the sadhakas Baba was directly questioning him whether it was proper of him to have entertained such a thought! He remained standing, benumbed and speechless. He now fully understood that He who is the Supreme Lord, One who is linked to our very pulse beat, catches all our innermost thoughts and feelings. Even a fleeting momentary thought in one's mind cannot be kept secret from Him.

"Tomáre lukáye kii karite pári... Práńer spandan yuga vivartan/ Sabai shona gopana kathá". This liila story clearly reveals that Baba is indeed closely linked to all creation, both collectively and individually. He is within our very life's breath, every passing moment, and so for Him there are no boundaries of time and space. Indeed, He is beyond the scope of time. The passage of time does not affect Him in the least. He is with each and every action of ours, and every incident and occurrence is known to Him, for He is always there as the silent Supreme Witnessing Entity. He knows all, whether an event took place a week back or a month back or years back. Indeed He knows all that took place in the hoary past. That is why what is said of Him in the words of the song is literally and profoundly true!

In the history of Ananda Marga the name of Shri Shashiranjan deserves to be written in golden letters. A scion of the distinguished Sahu family of Muzaffarpur in Bihar, Shashiranjanji was well endowed with wealth and learning. From a young age he had entered into the world of politics. He had joined the Congress, the then leading Political Party, and was one of its prominent members. He was also a respected member of Parliament. However, some years later, after he had come in contact with Baba, he was disillusioned by the Party's unethical policies which went against the norms of Dharma and morality. He ultimately left the Congress Party, and severed all connections with it.

“Práner Spandana Yuga Vivartana”

Shashi Babu was a devoted disciple of Baba, and strictly adhered to all His instructions. For him Baba's word was final. Only a few people belonging to the wealthy strata of society came in contact with Baba, and fewer still continued to remain within the organization. Shashiranjanji was one of those rare exceptions. Handsome and dignified in appearance, he was well-built, with a clear and light complexion. A large pair of light brown glasses framed a face that was full and pleasing, with a broad high brow that seemed

to be merging into his fast receding hairline. Always immaculately dressed in a hand-spun cotton dhoti, kurta and jacket, his personality commanded much respect. He could be easily spotted from afar, the most impressive of all the devotees gathered around Baba. Gifted with a good voice, he was an accomplished speaker, qualities that further enhanced his brilliant personality. Inspite of his accomplishments, he remained simple and unaffected, good humoured and friendly at all times. Shashiranjanji was a prominent and active member of the Marga and Baba had great regards for him. During his life Shashi Babu had had a lot of personal contact with Baba and had enjoyed His close association. The incident related in the following story is in connection with Shashi Babu.

Shashiranjanji had a grown up son, Kartik Babu, whose pet name was "Guddu". Baba too used to address him as "Guddu". One day Guddu was stricken by a strange and excruciating pain which caused a stiffening of his neck. He was normal when he went to sleep the night before, and on awakening the neck was painfully stiff. Under a friend's advice a hakim was consulted, but his treatment proved disastrous. The doctor turned out to be a quack, and as a result of his massages, Guddu's pain was aggravated and slowly spread to all other parts of his body. In fact his condition deteriorated, the pain became unbearable, and he became bed-ridden like a paralytic patient. There was almost no movement in his limbs and his state was pitiable. He became totally dependent on others, and two attendants were required to help him with his regular routine. Helpless and stiff, he had to be fed like a little child.

“Práner Spandana Yuga Vivartana”

The family, anxious and saddened by his sorry state, consulted all the prominent doctors in Patna. Medical examinations, tests and investigations began, treatment was given, but there was no improvement in his condition. It appeared as though there was no remedy for this terrible affliction and the whole family was in a state of despair. Overcome with hopelessness, some of his close relatives suggested to Shashi Babu that he should seek Baba's advice, and this suggestion was backed by some of the Margii brothers as well.

Shashi Babu declined to do so, saying, "Is there any need to tell Baba? After all nothing is hidden from Him. If it is His Grace, He Himself will bring up this matter. Why should we trouble Him unnecessarily?" His well-wishers, while agreeing that Baba was all-knowing, nevertheless felt that under the constraints of our human condition one had no alternative but to approach Him at the physical level. So they strongly advised Shashi Babu to personally raise this issue with Baba. Shashi Babu remained unconvinced and stuck to his earlier viewpoint. He continued to bear the burden of his son's illness and could not be persuaded to change his mind.

Now it so happened that Baba was in Delhi, and one evening He was preparing to leave His room to proceed on His field walk. Several Margii brothers and sisters were waiting outside Baba's room, hands folded, in expectation of His darshana. Shashi Babu was also waiting in line. Baba emerged from His room, and greeting all the Margiis, continued to proceed, even as He did namaskar to all the devotees. He passed by Shashiranjanji, went ahead, and then came back again, this time stopping in front of him and asked: "Shashiranjan! Tell me how is Guddu?"

Now that Baba Himself had inquired about Guddu, Shashi Babu felt no inhibition in talking to Him. Unhesitatingly he poured out all his anxieties and related everything to Baba. Having heard him carefully, Baba remained silent for a while and then said, "Alright Shashiranjan, do one thing, note down this address."

“Práner Spandana Yuga Vivartana”

Shashiranjan took out his diary and Baba dictated to him the detailed address of a doctor, one Shailendra Mukherjee by name, who lived in a small town in the Burdwan District of Bengal. Baba advised Shashi Babu to take Guddu to this doctor, adding that he would be able to cure him. Having said this, Baba moved on and continued on His way. Now that Baba had spoken to him and had paid heed to his problem, Shashiranjanji felt as though a great burden had fallen off his head. He felt instantly relieved of half his worries and anxieties.

Shashiranjanji knew full well that the Guru's word is precious and its worth cannot be measured in worldly terms. With this conviction, he forthwith made plans to travel to Burdwan. After making several inquiries he finally arrived at the right address. All along Shashi Babu had been thinking that Dr. Mukherjee must be a well-known specialist, though he himself had never heard about him.

However when he finally met with the doctor, all his presumptions proved wrong, and indeed he was in for a rude shock. He was confronted with a very different picture, that of an uncouth betel-chewing veritable peasant, in his filthy lungi and undershirt unabashedly spitting everywhere! Doubts arose in Shashi Babu's mind as he wondered as to how this ordinary doctor would be able

to help cure his son, when even the most prominent specialists of Patna had proven unsuccessful.

“Práner Spandana Yuga Vivartana”

However Shashi Babu managed to cast aside his sinking hopes, and renewing his faith in Baba, he approached the doctor and respectfully greeted him. He introduced himself to the doctor, giving him his background and then continued to relate in detail Guddu's medical history. Just as the honourable MP had received a rude shock on meeting with the doctor, so also, Dr. Shailendra Mukherjee was left speechless on meeting with Shashi Babu.
