On that fateful day, seventeen innocent monks and nuns of Ananda Marga were brutally murdered and burnt alive. This gory incident took place in broad daylight in a crowded market square. The state government turned a deaf ear to pleas for an inquiry into this heinous crime, and in fact sheltered the criminals.

Communist party members, bearing allegiance to the red flag, bore yet another grudge against the Proutists. The reason was that many senior and responsible party members, having been impressed by Proutist principles, left the Communist Party. They had chosen to discard their red robes for the saffron of the Proutist forum. Today there are many such persons, both amongst the sanyasiis and the active householder Margiis, who were once staunch and active members of the Marxist Party. It would not be proper to disclose their names and identities in the present context. However, the subject of the following story was one such person, whose name we cannot withhold. His name is Maheshwar Pandey and the story that follows relates his experience.

Pandeyji's Examination in the Pandal

Maheshwar Pandey hailed from Patna. He was a staunch Marxist with an unquestioning, almost blind faith in its principles. In those days of the "cold war" he was one of those Marxist Indians who would step into the street with an umbrella over his head if it happened to rain in Peking or Moscow. The mere mention of a cold wave in Shanghai or Leningrad would send shivers down his spine, in faraway India.

(This is one of the very common and popular satirical jibes that are directed against the Communist Party members in India. Their fanaticism and unquestioning acceptance of the red party political culture has inevitably invited much ridicule.)

By a stroke of good luck, Maheshwar Pandey happened to come in contact with the Marga. He undertook a thorough study of the lucid PROUT philosophy and drew his own conclusions. He realized that the Marxist principle of complete equality of all men went against the grain of practical reality. It was an altogether fictitious stand. Compared to it, the Proutist principles appeared to have enshrined high ideals that were both logical and practical to implement. Attracted by its principles, Maheshwar Pandey was thoroughly convinced of their integrity and efficacy. He began to take an active part in all the open seminars organized to propagate the PROUT philosophy.

In spite of his convictions and willing participation in PROUT activities, Maheshwar Pandey adamantly refused to take initiation. Moulded in the atheistic tradition, his mind was trained in logical reasoning and analysis. Intuition and the subtleties of Brahma Sadhana were hitherto alien concepts. His rationally inclined mind could very well appreciate PROUT's logical concepts but revolted against the idea of intuitive spiritual practice. His arguments and deliberations could not stand the test of reason and logic, and each time he had to admit his defeat. Even so, his tainted mind was unable to shed the red stigma of religious antagonism. It was a concept too deeply rooted in his consciousness. He undoubtedly considered Baba to be a great intellectual and a philosopher beyond compare. He had no hesitation in acknowledging the extraordinary caliber of Baba's erudition and learning. But he could not bring himself to accept and acknowledge Baba's role as Sadguru.

Pandeyji's Examination in the Pandal

His friend, who had introduced him to the Marga and to PROUT philosophy, would constantly urge and encourage him to take initiation. Ultimately, at the intellectual level he was prepared to admit that spiritual practice was one of the most vital aspects of human life, its fundamental need. In spite of this, he held himself back, refusing to be swayed and allured by the gift of sadhana. The ghost of "atheism," the hallmark of communist theory, was still entrenched in the inner recess of his mind. Some of these atheistic samskaras, sowed in the past, were preventing him from taking confident steps on the path of dharma. Very often he would give in to the persistent urges of his friend, and agree to take initiation. But at the very last minute he would bring up some excuse and evade the situation. This game of snakes and ladders continued and many opportunities slipped by. For a long while he carried on with this game of hide and seek.

Maheshwar Pandey often used to visit the Marga office in Patna. It was situated on Exhibition Road (Brajkishore Path). He had established good contacts with several avadhutas and took a keen interest in any discussions on PROUT philosophy. However, whenever the topic of initiation surfaced, he would at once refuse and reject the suggestion. Diiksha energizes the inner spirit and motivates one to take firm steps in the direction of one's goal. On account of his own indecision, reluctance and reservation, Maheshwar Pandey deprived himself of these wonderful benefits and blessings. Deep-rooted prejudices had taken an icy hold over his heart and mind—so much so that even the warmth and glow of satsanga could not cause these long frozen prejudices to thaw. But for how long could one stem this thaw?

Pandeyji's Examination in the Pandal

One night Maheshwar Pandey had a strange dream. He dreamt that he was standing outside the Marga office in Patna. A rickshaw approaches the entrance to the office and an avadhuta Dada alights from it. Coming towards him, the Dada engages in a long conversation with him. This is the very Dada with whom he would often have long drawn discussions and arguments on topics of philosophy and spirituality. After talking to him for a while, the Dada grasps Pandeyji's hand and forcefully drags him into a vacant room in the office premise. He tells Pandeyji, "Today I will not let you go without initiating you." This was only a dream. The night passed away and the dream was over.

The next day, as per his routine, Pandeyji left for the office on Exhibition Road. He was standing outside the office when suddenly the very same Dada who had appeared in his dream alighted from a rickshaw. The Dada approached him, and repeating the very words he had spoken to him in the dream, grasped Pandeyji's hand and led him into the office. In exactly the same commanding tone of voice the Dada said, "Today I will not let you off without initiating you." Pandeyji was quite bewildered. The astonishing similarity between his dream and reality was in itself unbelievable. Added to that was Dada's forceful insistence and authoritative, almost intimidating tone of voice. He could not help but relent. In short, through the Lord's compassion and grace, so mysteriously showered on Pandeyji, he was finally convinced and ready to take confident steps towards his destined goal. He quickly took initiation from the Dada.

Soon after taking initiation, Pandeyji was indeed fortunate to get Baba's darshana. He was finally able to meet in person the one whom he had come to regard as the repository of all knowledge and learning, the one before whom he had no hesitation in surrendering himself, in acknowledgement of His immense erudition. In his very first meeting, Baba asked Pandeyji as to why he had been running away from Him for so long. Why had he been so hesitant about meeting Him?

Pandeyji's Examination in the Pandal

It is said that Maheshwar Pandey could find no words to reply to Baba's queries. His first, emotional meeting with Baba had a great impact on Pandeyji. His doubts were now dispelled and a new-found conviction filled his heart and mind. He became particular about sadhana practices and made good progress. In all sincerity, he looked upon Baba as his Guru and compassionate saviour. However, there was one aspect of Baba which he still could not accept and acknowledge, and that was His role as Taraka Brahma. His argumentative mind was moulded and reared in the socialistic climate of uncompromising atheism. Such a mind could not accept or digest the idea of divine omniscience, when applied to Baba. He believed that Baba's devotees, in their enthusiasm and anxiousness to spread His mission, had imagined and exaggerated these divine virtues. It seemed to him that the devotees, out of overwhelming love and devotion for Baba, had conjured up these divine qualities.

It was the year 1968 and a Dharma Mahacakra was organized at Allahabad. Maheshwar Pandey decided to attend the function. At the mahacakra he happened to meet with a senior householder acarya, Vishwamohanji. As they got talking the same contentious topic of Baba's omniscience surfaced. At the end of the discussion, Pandeyji as usual remained rooted in his stand and refused to accept that such things could be possible. The ardent devotee Vishwamohanji replied, "Brother! Of course it is but natural for you to think so. Why should you accept what others tell you? Why don't you test this out for yourself? Only then will you know whether Baba truly is the knower of all things past, present and future. Examine Him yourself and find out whether He is the true and eternal witness to the tiniest individual living mind. After all, the results of experiments conducted in one's own laboratory are the most satisfying. Why should you taste the remnants from another's plate? Instead, cook your own food and enjoy it!"

Pandeyji's Examination in the Pandal

Pandeyji was quite taken up by this advice, so much so that he promptly decided to carry out his own experiment during the proceedings of that Dharma Mahacakra itself. He was determined to find out for himself all that he had heard about Baba's omniscient nature. He thought that he would conduct his own experiment, make his own independent inquiry and draw his own conclusions. This way he would unerringly sift the true from the false.

In one of the general darshanas held in the course of the Dharma Mahacakra program, Pandeyji was seated at the far end of the pandal. Soon the entire pandal came alive with the echo of conch-shells and the resounding cries of "Param Pita Baba ki jai," "Vishva Pita Baba ki jai," "Mahasambhuti Baba ki jai." The cynosure of all eyes, Baba entered the pandal, and greeting all the devotees with a namaskara, He sat down on the sacred seat. Once again He greeted the sadhakas, His hands folded in the namaskara gesture, slowly turning from right to left, His eyes brimming with compassion. He seemed to be showering His grace on one and all. After this, He settled down to deliver His discourse.

The proceedings began with the singing of devotional bhajanas. The entire pandal was engulfed in the joyous wave of devotional sentiment. The sadhakas, charged with emotion and feeling, dived deep into the waters of this devotional-Ganga and were drenched by its holy current. However, there was one person who was unable to partake of this wonderful experience. His immature mind, inclined towards rationality, held him back, and he remained confused, stranded on the dry banks of the holy river. As long as the analytical mind seeks security on the dry banks of logic and reason, it cannot plunge into the sweet waters of devotion and flow with its current. Such a mind is bound to the shore by the chains of worldly knowledge. How then can it taste the devotional butter churned midstream by the swiftly flowing holy waters?

Pandeyji's Examination in the Pandal

In his crude and calculating mind, Pandeyji said to himself, "At this very moment, I will do namaskara to Baba, in the very midst of this congregation. If Baba is omniscient, He will likewise return my greeting." Just as the thought was seeded in Pandeyji's mind, his hands moved into action and thus began his examination of Baba. The examinee responded in a trice with a prompt counter-namaskara to his honourable examiner. The examiner was astounded.

However, a thorough examiner does not consider the results of a single test to be conclusive. He thought that this could have been purely coincidental. So once more he did namaskara and once again he received a return greeting from the dais. In spite of this, Pandeyji remained unsure and unconvinced by the 'results' of his experiment, and tossed it off as another coincidence. He got up, and went in front towards the left side of the stage on which Baba was seated, and he

sat down amongst the avadhutas and volunteers. This time he was not seated directly in front of Baba. From his new position, Pandeyji once more did namaskara to Baba. With a smile on His face, Baba turned to His left and responded with a namaskara to His examiner.

The respected examiner was taken aback; in fact, he was quite agitated. He got up and once again walked back to his previous seat at the far end of the pandal. It seemed as though the examiner himself was now on the burner. The results of his self-conducted experiment were proving too hot for him to handle! Even so, his analytical mind refused to acknowledge the facts of the situation and was unable to accept the truth of what it had encountered. Each time he tried to dismiss the 'proofs' of his experiments by labeling them as "mere coincidence." In this way he tried his utmost to pass off the truth as false.

Pandeyji's Examination in the Pandal

Suddenly, he spotted Acarya Vishwamohanji, who was seated in one corner of the pandal. Pandeyji got up and went and sat next to the acarya. He apprised Vishwamohanji of the 'tests' he had conducted and simultaneously commented that all the incidents could have been coincidental. Acarya Vishwamohanji deliberately went along with what Pandeyji had to say, and pretended to agree with him wholeheartedly. He said to Pandeyji, "Certainly they could have been coincidental. You can never be sure until you make a thorough investigation, one which is foolproof in every way. So conduct another exam, indeed do so over and over again." Vishwamohanji had decided that the time had come for Pandeyji to undergo some agonizing soul-searching. Paradise can only be gained by undergoing mortal agonies. One has to die to oneself in order to pass through the heavenly gates.

This time Pandeyji conjured up a novel 'test paper' and began his examination. He had often heard that Baba could immediately pick up every thought wave that crosses one's mind. He thought to himself that he would mentally pose a question to Baba, even as He was giving His discourse. Then he would wait and see how Baba, in spite of being absorbed in His speech, would yet reply to his question. The subject of the discourse was, "Karmeshana Shakti Vichitrah."

Pandeyji thought to himself that he would pose a question which would have no relevance to the topic of the discourse. He prepared the question in his mind and mentally posed it to Baba. He asked, "Tell me, is it possible for a human being to maintain his body over a long period of time, say for a hundred or more years? Further, is it possible for a human being to retain in his next life the knowledge and wisdom accumulated in his previous birth?"

Pandeyji's Examination in the Pandal

When he ended his questions, Baba, diverging from the topic of His discourse, said, "Yes, it is certainly possible for a human being to maintain his body in good condition over a long period of time. In the remote and sanctified regions of the Himalayas, there are many saintly souls, yogiis who are more than a hundred years old, still alive and engrossed in their sadhana practices." Continuing to answer the questions, Baba further said, "It is possible for highly evolved siddha sadhakas to preserve and retain their knowledge and wisdom, completely intact, through several births and lifetimes." Having promptly concluded His exam, Baba immediately returned to the topic of His discourse. The general listeners could not grasp the secret of this sudden divergence from the main topic of the discourse. However, it may be said that devotees always remain intoxicated and transfixed by the very darshana of Baba. Hence they did not give a second thought to what Baba had just spoken. Ecstatic with devotion, they continued to gaze intently at Him.

On the other hand, Pandeyji, who was seated at the far end of the pandal, had suffered a crushing defeat on the battlefield of his 'war' with Baba. He threw away all his weapons. Suddenly he found himself released from the constraining bondage of worldly wisdom. He was now able to dive deeply into the swiftly flowing Ganga of devotion. Mentally he surrendered himself to His divine Guru and bathed His Feet with the tears that flowed profusely from his eyes. He was now fully convinced that what Baba had shown was beyond the capacity of a limited mind. This could only be possible through a superhuman mind. He had no more doubts about Baba and His omniscient powers. Overwhelmed with wonder and delight, he sat, staring intently at Baba, gazing at Him, unable to take his eyes off Him.

Nashto mohah smrtir labdhá tava prasádán mayácyuta,
Sthito 'smi gatasandehah karishye vacanam tava.
("By Your grace, O Krishna, my infatuation has been destroyed, and my senses regained. I rise, all doubts removed; I shall act as per Your word.")

Mother Yashoda of Jamalpur

One cannot predetermine a standard yardstick for a devotee's mode of expression towards his or her cherished goal, ista, nor is it possible to compare the varying expressions of one devotee with another. The mode of expression depends on the mental level, the spiritual level and more than anything else on the samskaras of one's past life. A few wish to cultivate the relationship of a friend with their ista, some cultivate a filial relationship, while others that of a lover. Jaki rahi bhavana jaisi, prabhu murat dekhi tina vaisi ("Depending on one's particular bhavan, mental feeling, one sees the image of the Lord accordingly").

Every devotee wishes to see his beloved Lord in the mind according to the previously established form he has given Him; no other form appeals to him. It is said that when Krishna appeared before the greatest of bhaktas, Tulsidas, he refused his darshana saying, "Tulsi bows his head only when He bears the bow and arrow in hand," referring to his favourite image of Lord Rama. Even the amazingly attractive, charming Krishna himself could not seduce Tulsi. Night and day, in his eyes was embedded the sole image of Rama, the son of Dasharath. He could not see his ista in any other form.

The relationship of Parama Purusa with the human being is a personal one. Whatever may be the form in which He appears to the collective body, one wishes to take Him into his heart through one's own personal vision of Him. One does not accept a form determined by others. In this lies the uniqueness of devotion, in this lies the special pride of a devotee.

shriináthe jánakiináthe cribhede paramátmani,
tathápi mama sarvasvam rámah kamalalocnnah.

Mother Yashoda of Jamalpur

("Whether called Vishnu, or Rama, Siita's lord, there is no distinction as to the Supreme Self. Nonetheless, Rama of the lotus eyes is everything to me.")

Baba has also said that the closeness and proximity one feels, the sense of oneness and intimacy one feels with one's personal ista, cannot be felt with Nirguna Brahma; In Tantra, too, the central place that Taraka Brahma holds is all important. Neither is Nirguna Brahma, nor is Saguna Brahma, the entity who guides one who seeks moksha onto the path of truth; one's only guardian, the helmsman of life's boat, is the personal ista, Taraka Brahma, the Sadguru. One accepts the Sadguru alone as the essence of essences. The Guru alone is Brahma, the Guru alone is Vishnu, the Guru is Maheshvara, the Guru himself is Parama Brahma. Brahmaiva gurur ekah náparah. ("Brahma alone is the one Guru, none other.") One can only pay homage, such as in "Tavadmvyam jagadguro tubhyam evn samarpaye," to the Sadguru, and to none else. It is said in the Kularnava Tantra:

dhyánamúlam guror múrtih pújámúlam guroh padam,
mantramúlam guror vákyam mokshamúlam guroh krpa.

("The root of dhyána is the form of the Guru; the root of worship, His feet. The root of mantra is the word of the Guru, the root of salvation His grace.")

Mother Yashoda of Jamalpur

As long as there isn't a complete feeling of intimacy and "mine-ness" between the devotee and the Lord, between the one who meditates and the object of meditation, the river of devotion remains dry; there is no possibility of submergence in the absorption of bhava, nor is there the possibility of sipping the surging waters of bhava.

What can a devotee do with a dry, waterless stream? Neither can one wash, nor rinse oneself in it. The devotee cannot live without devotion; devotion is the devotee's water of life. Just as a fish cannot live without water, a devotee cannot live without devotion. It is said:

bhaktir bhágavatah sevn' bhaktih premasvnrfpinii,
bhaktir ánandarúpá ca bhaktir bhaktasya jiivanam.

("Devotion is service to the Lord; devotion is the true essence of love. Devotion takes the form of bliss, and devotion is the very life of the devotee.")

Mother Yashoda of Jamalpur

The devotee always wishes for the proximity of his ista; he wishes to become one-pointed in his devotions, and desires to live his life with complete faith in the ista. The Lord Himself is hungry for one-pointedness of devotion; He too cannot stand duality. During his lifetime, through the narrative of His own life, Baba gave us examples of the confounding snares of the illusory world that a sense of duality can lead one into. Not by philosophy and discussion, but rather by

writing a brilliant chapter of His own life, did Baba teach us this lesson. Those lessons not fully internalized by the devotee through the medium of discourses, or through svadhyaya, the Mahasambhuti has to choreograph and direct himself on the stage of his own life. "Liiladhara" Baba did just this. Hamrahi was an insignificant part of his liila, his play.

For a short period of time, the practitioners within the Marga faced a test of their discipline, their one-pointedness towards their ista, and their surrender. In the ancient traditions, more than one history of devotion to the ista has been written. The sadhakas of Ananda Marga had walked the path of these very traditions. As per Ananda Marga's philosophy and its system of meditation, it was crucial to forge the one-pointedness of all devotees in the tradition of non-duality.

During the short period of this test, the minds of the traditional devotees were divided in their attraction to two istas. One attraction was towards Baba's unusually captivating form of Shiva, while the other was towards His wife Shrimati Uma's affectionate form of Shakti. In those days, his wife Uma was known as Marga Mata, "Mother of the Marga." In Dharma Mahacakras, in which Baba would give his darshana and blessings in the form of the Parama Guru, there, before his arrival, Marga Mata began to give her darshana and blessings as well.

Mother Yashoda of Jamalpur

The devotees were torn between the attractive force of these two magnets. At times they were attracted to one side, at other times they were attracted to the other side. Even if the attraction towards one did not create repulsion towards the other, at times the scales were tipped in favor of Margaguru, and at times in favour of Marga Mata. Hamrahi too became a victim of this erroneous duality; he was a pawn tossed between two divine entities. Due to maya's play, for a brief while Hamrahi became mad for the love of the affectionate Mother's divine attraction and radiance. He did not even wish to see Baba. Perhaps this was because Baba's role as the giver of causeless endless love was mixed with his role as one who punishes, and who controls through the severity of discipline, while in Mother's presence there was only love, pure untainted love. As far as possible Hamrahi preferred to sit at the feet of Mother. She became a strong stimulus for his divided love.

However, in order to end this dual sentiment of the infatuating attraction of Uma, Maheshwara Baba used the rod in his role as guardian and controller, such that both mother and son became the victim of an awful disaster. Despite the physical warnings, when the duality of Maya did not end, Baba let loose his brahmastra, his sudarshan cakra, snatching away all of Shrimati Uma's majesty, taking away from her all her power. Like an expanded balloon is deflated with the mere touch of a pin, so also was the Shakti, the mother Uma's majesty, lost. For the sake of the well-being of the world, "Uma-Maheshvara," as before, became separated.

In order to explain along with a clear example the meaning of Shaktih sá shivasya shaktih ("The Supreme Operative Principle, Shakti, is Shiva's Shakti"), in order to firmly establish advaita philosophy, and to consecrate meditation on a single ista, Baba himself had thrown his net of maya, and He Himself drew it in. The essence of the matter is that in order to gain the final station, a single ideational form of the ista, a feeling of oneness, one-pointed devotion, complete surrender and an undivided expression of intimacy are the first and last, most crucial and inalienable principles.

Mother Yashoda of Jamalpur

In devotion, one-pointedness is obligatory. The feelings of prapatti and complete surrender are also essential. Ámi yantra, tumi yantri, ámi ghar, tumi gharni, ámi rath, tumi rathi, má, yeman cáláo temani cali ("I am the instrument, you are the one who works it; I am the house, you the householder; I am the chariot, you the charioteer. Mother, I'll move the way you move me"): such should be the sentiment of prapatti. And the feeling of complete surrender should be such: tere phulon se bhi pyar, tere kanton se bhi pyar, jo bhi dena cahe, de de karatar, duniya ke taranahar ("I am in love with your flowers, I am in love with your thorns. Whatever you wish to give me, give me, O Lord, the saviour of this world"). Because of undivided love, the devotee does not wish to share his internal Lord with others. He is his, completely his, only his. He does not wish to raise his eyes and look at another, nor is it permitted for his ista to look at anybody else. Nainan andar av tun, nain dhanpi tohi leu, na main dekhun aur ko, na tohi dekhan deu ("Come into my eyes, then only will I close them. I do not look at another, nor do I allow you to do so"): this alone is the devotee's inalienable right, and is the completely surrendered one-pointedness of mind which ultimately brings him union with the divine. Then, tal mile nadi ke jal men, nadi mile ságar men, sagar mile kaunse jal men, koi jáne na, ("The waters of the lake flow into the river; the river merges into the sea. What does the sea merge into? No one knows"); and how can anyone know? Once the devotee is united with his ista, his identity is obliterated. How can he

possibly know what happens to him next? Lali mere lal ki jita dekho tita lal | lali dekhan main gayi, main bhi ho gayi lal ("I see the rosiness of my rose-hued beloved wherever I look. I went to gaze upon his ruddy looks, and myself became rosy").

Mother Yashoda of Jamalpur

The expressions of the devotee with his beloved ista take on many colours and forms, many dimensions. It is not possible to limit these. Devotees' perspectives vary, the modes of their expressions are different. Some scream in pain, while others believe in rona ho to ro cupke-cupke, ansu na bahe, avaz na ho ("If one has to cry, then cry quietly, such that tears do not flow, and no sound of sobbing is heard."). Some are calm and controlled, while others make loud sounds of "hum." A few become silent stones, others begin to tremble like the leaves of the winter month of Magha. Some become like the desert keeping their expansive love within them, others flow incessantly like the waterfalls of the rainy month Shravana.

Some see their closest friend in their ista, others see their final shelter in the fatherly form of the ista. Some are engrossed in service-mindedness, while others are lost in gopi bhava. Rama is one; but for Lakshmana and Bharat, he was brother, for Sita he was beloved husband, for Kaushalya he was son, for Hanuman he was master, and for Vibhishana he was friend. In this manner, every sadhaka, depending on samskaras, sees a different form in his goal. The ista is one, but the visions of Him different. Every role played by a devotee is of equal importance to that played by another; no devotee is superior to any other.

Mother Yashoda of Jamalpur

Draupadi's sakhi bhava, and Yashoda's motherly love – both of their identities had their own place, each of their roles equally important. In Vrindavan, Draupadi played no part, while during the time of the Mahabharata, Yashoda had no place. But Krishna was equally beloved to each of them, equally internalized; both their hearts were equally suffused with the sentiment of devotion for Krishna. During the time of building the bridge, while the role of Nala and Nila was important, the industry, the hard work and faith of the squirrel could not be replaced by any other character of the Ramayana. Devaki's motherly role, and her love mixed with admonishment, were different from Yashoda's caring, affectionate motherly love, but yet the two are not different. In terms of moral lessons, Sudama bore sakha bhava for Krishna, while Guru Sandipani was the guru of the Jagadguru. Every character plays out his role on the stage of liila in lieu of their own samskaras. In the enactment of his liila every character joyfully and painstakingly plays the role designated by the ista.

Despite the numerous types of bhakti-bhava, four main types of expressions of devotion have been established in Tantra. They are: 1) Dasya bhava 2) Sakhya bhava 3) Radha bhava 4) Vatsalya bhava. In the case of the first bhava, the devotee has a keen desire to remain the servant of the Lord. The sadhika Mira, whose name is indeed blessed, says:

prabhuji mhane cakar rakhoji
cakar rahasyan, bag lagasyan
nita utha darshan pasyan

("Lord, keep me as your servant. While your servant, I will plant your garden. Daily will I wake and have your darshan.")

Mother Yashoda of Jamalpur

Another in surrendered dasya bhava, says:

main gulam, main gulam, main gulam tero,
tu divan, tu divan, tu divan mero.

("I am a slave, I am a slave, I am a slave of yours. You are the master, you the master, you are the master of mine.")

In every situation, by some means or another, these devotees wish to spend their lives in the service of their Lord alone. Hanuman's dasya bhava in the Ramaliila is unique, incomparable, unparalleled. His sole aim was the service of Rama. Rama kaju kinhen binu, mohi kahan vishram ("Without performing Rama's work, how can I find rest?") was his mahamantra. He did not leave any stone unturned in the service of his ista, even if it meant that he had to put his own life in grave danger. The amount of love that Rama has for Bharat – he has just as much for Hanuman, the son of the wind. In Lakshmana too we mainly see dasya bhava, even though there is a certain amount of brotherly love, due to the fact that he is Rama's brother.

Mother Yashoda of Jamalpur

In terms of dasya bhava, the best roles allotted to devotees were to Arjuna and Draupadi. The mutual sakha bhava shared by the Lord and Arjuna, and Draupadi's sakhi bhava for Krishna were both unique. In the same way Sudama's sakha bhava for Krishna is also an incomparable model.

Radha bhava or gopi bhava is the sweetest bhava of the world of bhakti bhava. This is the bhava of the blissful attraction of love. Baba had said that there was no woman by the name of Radha. Radha is a

bhava, which was very beloved of Krishna. He in whom radha bhava is awakened becomes full of thoughts of Krishna. Mohan's flute unwittingly draws him close to Him. It becomes totally impossible for him to live far from Kanha's sweet attraction. As soon as the flute-player began playing his flute, Radha ran barefooted to Him. This kind of loving attraction is of a very high order. The bhava that the Gopas and Gopis of Vrindavan felt for Krishna was radha bhava. The one in whom this bhava was best kindled became the envy of all other Gopas and Gopis. Among the Gopis there was a keen competition to outdo each other in experiencing this bhava. In this bhava, there is great intimacy, extreme proximity. Once one is established in this bhava, it is not possible for the devotee and his object of ideation to remain apart for long; their swift union is inevitable.

This is why this bhava is the highest level of devotion. Every moment, the mind says with Ramakrishna Paramahansa, dubo dubo re man rúpaságare ("Drown, drown, O mind, in the ocean of beautiful form"). In this bhava the last established level is that of aham brahmásmi ("I am Brahma"). Prem gali ati sankari, ta men do na samay ("Love's lane is too narrow. Two cannot fit into it.") In this state, even if the unit mind wishes, it cannot remain apart from the macrocosmic mind. The mind's condition becomes that of shudhu tár-i pine chute jáy ("To him alone does the mind flee"). This is also called gopa bhava: gopáyate ynh sa gopnh ("one who behaves like a herder is a gopa"). This is the ultimate condition of restlessness, whose final end is found in ultimate union.

Mother Yashoda of Jamalpur

The fourth bhava is vatsalya bhava. This bhava is also of two types. One kind is that in which the sadhaka views the Lord as his mother or father, and constantly wishes to remain ensconced like a child in the security of his bliss-giving lap; he does not wish to remain away from his lap for even a second. Even if he is away from His lap in order to play for a while, he is like a bird on a ship that returns back to the ship no matter how far it flies. He does not wish to go far from the security of the lap of his Universal Father, or that of his Universal Mother. As far as I understand, most Ananda Margiis bear this sort of bhava for Baba, because Baba Himself, has linked Himself, and His name, to this mundane sense.

The second kind of vatsalya bhava is that in which one's own motherly love is awakened, i.e. one sees one's ista as one's son. This bhava is awakened in very few devotees. In Krishna's time Devaki and Yashoda bore this bhava. The bhakta-poet Surdas has given a complete characterization of Yashoda's vatsalya bhava, which was beyond all bhavas. From main nahin makhan khayo ("I did not eat the butter") to mata yashoda le ura anga lagayo ("Mother Yashoda clasping him to her breast took Him into her arms"), Sur's literature is filled with vatsalya rasa. Surdas composed a beautiful, lively picture of vatsalya bhava and vatsalya rasa. When he spread that beautiful cloud of vatsalya rasa over the literary world, it was almost as though the leading intellectuals found a new rasa among the nine rasas, and they began to speak of including it among the nine. Also, his literature was the sweetest expression of bhakti.

Mother Yashoda of Jamalpur

The Jamalpur chapter of Baba's self-determined earthly life was an incomparable chapter in the sweetness of bhakti bhava. The devotees who emerged during that period were indeed very fortunate: they received the unique golden opportunity to experience Baba's sincere love, to interact with Him in a fearless and free manner, to discuss things with him in a straightforward manner. To them Baba was a member of their family, and they were a part of Baba's own family. Such were their mutual, familial relationships, their supreme feelings of intimacy with Baba: that hearing of this the devotees of the Parthasarathi era burn with envy. As much as he was informal, near and sweet in his role as Vrajaraja during the Jamalpur era, in the Parthasarathi period, even though inwardly he was love incarnate, outwardly he remained very distant, formal, bounded by rules, strictly disciplining, often carrying out harsh punishment. His Parthasarathi role can be likened to a coconut or bel fruit – sweet on the inside, but very hard on the outside.

In Jamalpur the devotees used to consider Baba to be an inseparable part of their everyday lives. In that era also the expression of every devotee was different. One of Baba's unique devotees used to interact with Him as though He was her son. She used to consider Him to be her son, and would love Him like Devaki, like Yashoda. The Aulipur family of Jamalpur was very close to Baba, very beloved of His. Every member of that family was a unique devotee of Baba's. The reminiscences of Ramakhilawan Singh of that very family constitute an incomparable chapter of Namami Kalyanasundaram. Other members of the family were also Baba's beloved devotees. Each devotee had his own special attitude to worshipping Him, his own expression of devotion. Krantiji was one

of the members of this family; as her name suggests she was truly a 'revolutionary' in the world of devotion. Krantiji's devotion was incomparable. She was completely established in madhura bhava or gopi bhava. Whenever she would see Baba, she would be overwhelmed by bhava; every limb of her body would begin to tremble.

Mother Yashoda of Jamalpur

Ramatanukaji was another unique member of the same family. She was the wife of Ramakhilavanji. Everyone in the organization used to call her Mother Ramatanukaji, and her rank was equivalent to Yashoda of Krishna's times. She would shower affection on Baba like a mother. Even though Baba was not her biological child, the motherly love she bore for Baba was certainly impelled by her past samskaras. Baba also did not hinder her vatsalya bhava in any way. Even though from a mundane standpoint it may have seemed nonsensical, Baba would behave like a child before Mother Ramatanuka. Ma Ramatanuka's heart was filled to the brim with vatsalya. It is very strange indeed, that He for whom most sadhakas would bear love mixed with respect, as for a father, Mother Ramatanuka would behave towards as her innocent little boy. Most people would be very surprised to see that whenever she would speak to Baba, she would speak as though she was instructing him. Like a son, Baba too would carry out her instructions faithfully, so that her role as a mother would always be upheld. If any instruction was not carried out as per her wishes, Mother Ramatanuka would unhesitatingly scold Him. Baba would also tolerate her admonishments like an innocent child. There is no doubt that there was not the least bit of ill-will in her admonishments; her scolding of Baba was always laden with great love. The admonishments were an expression of her unique motherly love, exactly like the scoldings of Yashoda.

Once a Margii from Surajgadh came to see Baba for the first time. Like the other people of his village he was in the habit of consuming ganja (marijuana) communally. Even the blanket he had covered himself with was exuding a strong smell of ganja. He had been initiated, but was not able to free himself from this tamasika habit. He sat down in the darshana room, along with all the others waiting to have Baba's darshana. Baba's discourse began. Ramatanukaji was also present in the room. When the discourse was completed, Baba got ready to leave. At that moment, this man quickly got up to touch Baba's feet. Baba, while still sitting on the cot, quickly retracted His feet, and did not give him an opportunity to take the dust of His feet.

Mother Yashoda of Jamalpur

Seeing this, Mother Ramatanuka was agitated; in a loud voice, she said, "If there is partiality even in Your court, then where will the fallen ones go? If they do not get shelter even at Your feet then where can they find refuge? Have You not come down to this earth to elevate the immoralists? Then why don't you give this poor man an opportunity to touch Your feet? Whoever comes into your shelter should get an appropriate place at Your auspicious feet, even if fallen or degraded. If they do not have a place, then it is not appropriate to call You Patita-pavana (the one who purifies the sinful). Put Your feet down, and give him a chance to pay his salutations at Your feet." Like an obedient son, Baba placed His feet on the ground, and gave the villager a chance to touch His feet. Such was Ramatanukaji's place of position and power. Baba used to respect her as His mother, and obey her as a son.

Often Ramatanukaji would send from her home something that she had cooked for Baba; at other times she would bring the dish herself. Baba had laid down a strict rule: no devotee could send anything to his house. But this rule did not apply to Ramatanukaji. Baba had told Thakurma (Abha Ma, Baba's laukika mother, Abharani Sarkar) that if anything were to come from Ramatanukaji's home, she should not refuse it. Moreover, he had also said that if any dish was sent by Ramatanukaji then she should certainly serve it at meal time. Ramatanuka Ma would sometimes send such dishes to Baba's home, and at other times to the jagrti. No matter how busy Baba would be in the jagrti, Ramatanukaji would somehow intervene in his busy program and manage to feed Baba whatever she had made. Baba would also not become irritated with her. Relishing the food, he would take his time eating it, just as Krishna had once eaten from the hands of Vidura's wife.

Mother Yashoda of Jamalpur

Yes, exactly as He had once eaten from the hands of Vidura's wife. Parthasarathi had not eaten bananas, but banana peels from the hands of Vidura's wife, with great relish. Ramatanuka Ma, carried away by bhava, would sometimes make a hodge-podge of her cooking. She would sometimes even forget to put salt in the food, or else she would put too much salt. But there would not be as much as a slight crease on Baba's forehead; he would relish everything she had made, enjoying His food without reservation. Ramatanukaji would sometimes make a

sherbet for him without sugar. That too Baba would drink up without any complaint, without turning up his nose, but rather relishing it as though it were sweet sugarcane juice.

Whatever was served with love He would accept. Even if by mistake an ant had fallen into the sherbet, he would not turn it away. Ramatanuka Ma would know of her mistakes on her return home when she would have the remaining food or drink as prasad. The next day she would herself come to Baba and say, partly guilty, partly complaining, "Yesterday, there was no salt at all in what I fed you!"

Baba would remark rather surprised, "Is that so?" Baba was not hungry for food, but for love; he was not thirsty for sherbet but for devotional bhavana. Through bhavana alone his hunger and thirst would be satiated. Such was Ramatanuka Ma's bhava-drenched love, her love filled with deep affection, her brimming, overflowing vatsalya! When she would sometimes forget to add sugar, and would complain the next day to Baba for not informing her about it, Baba would say, "Mother! There is such sweetness in your hands that whatever you give me is sweet to me; my mind becomes satiated."

Mother Yashoda of Jamalpur

Once Ramatanukaji wished to feed Baba with pedas (milk sweets) she had herself made. It is said that in His childhood, apart from butter, Krishna was fond of pedas. Mother Yashoda would often feed him with sweet pedas. With this in mind, Jamalpur's Mother Yashoda made pedas for Baba with great enthusiasm. That day she took especial care to use all the ingredients in well-measured amounts, so as to make the pedas especially delicious. Every time, whatever would remain she would distribute as prasad, or bring back home. That day she decided that she would feed every one of the pedas to Baba, and leave none uneaten by Him.

It was a Sunday. With great affection she arranged the pedas in a brass dish, and entered the jagrti. Baba completed His discourse. Ramatanuka Ma immediately got up and presented the pedas to Baba, saying, "Today, I have made a special dish for you, something that you greatly love. Today, you will have to finish everyone of them; I will not allow you to leave a single one uneaten."

Baba asked, "What have you brought, Mother?"

Ramatanukaji said, "pedas!"

Mother Yashoda of Jamalpur

Baba said, "Oh, pedas! I love pedas! But Mother, not today; if I eat anything here today, I will not be able to eat at home, and if I do not eat at home, then Thakurma will be upset. I love pedas, but not today. One day I will come having not eaten anything, with an empty stomach, and will certainly eat the pedas you make. But, not today, not at all!"

That day Baba was behaving like a stubborn child. But was Ramatanuka Ma going to listen to what He had to say?

She said, "Baba! You will certainly have to eat the pedas today."

Ultimately, Baba accepted to taste one peda and taking one from the dish, ate it. "Oho! How sweet! How tasty! I have never in my entire life tasted pedas such as these!" Baba was not relishing the pedas themselves, but the bhava-filled stubbornness with which she was serving them. Tasting her obstinacy that was laced with vatsalya, Baba was filling His stomach.

Mother Yashoda of Jamalpur

Mother said, "Have more!"

Pointing to His stomach, Baba said, "No, Ma! No more today!" But Mother was not about to listen; she continued to hold onto her demand. Finally, Baba melted and said, "Alright! You feed me with your own hands." Ramatanuka Ma picked out a peda with her hands, and asked Baba to open His mouth. Baba opened His mouth, but the peda remained as it was in Ramatanukaji's hands. In dazzlement, she became almost like a statue, as though she had gone into bhava samadhi. After a while, she fell down where she stood. The other devotees were left wondering as to what had happened. No one understood what had suddenly overtaken her, how it had happened. Absent-mindedly, Baba left for his home. Hours later, when she returned to normal, when she regained consciousness, then people asked her, "Ramatanuka Ma! What happened to you? Why? How?" She was not able to respond to the incessant stream of questions. For a long time, she kept crying in the grip of bhava. Wordless, silent tears, only tears, nothing other than tears.

After a while, when the passion of bhava decreased, she said that she had experienced that day the very experience that Mother Yashoda had had when Krishna had opened His mouth. "When He opened His mouth to eat the peda, I saw the entire universe in His mouth. The sun and the moon, the uncountable stars, meteors and galaxies – I saw them all! The entire cosmos was dancing in His mouth, the constellations of the solar system, one by one were orbiting in His mouth."

After that divine experience, Jamalpur's Mother Yashoda always remained somewhat abnormal. On hearing Baba's name she would become extremely emotional; she would lose herself in bhava; she would

Mother Yashoda of Jamalpur

become vibrated, and would begin to tremble. In this manner, throughout her life she always remained absorbed in bhava; she would often be lost in the liila of the Lord of liila. Eventually, the Lord of liila, by the lure of his liila, took this loving mother, utterly absorbed in His liila into His lovely lap.

---

náham vedair na tapasá na dánena na cejyayá,
shakya evamvidho drashtum drashtaván asi mám yathá.
bhaktyá tv ananyayá shakya aham evamvidho 'rjuna.
jnátum drashtum ca tattvena praveshtum ca paramtapa.
("Not through study of the Vedas, nor by penance, donations, or sacrifice, can I be seen in the way that you see me, O Arjuna. By exclusive devotion I may be known, seen, and entered truly, O fierce warrior.")

Seminars, the Spread of Knowledge, and the Aim of Sadhana

Baba compared jnana, knowledge, to a leaf-plate from which food has been partaken. The usefulness of jnana is only upto the point that samvit shakti, the power of consciousness, is awakened. Once this shakti is roused, consciousness has been awakened in the mind, discretion has been developed, and right understanding has been aroused, it is almost as though one has finished one's meal. Just as the only place for the used leaf-plate, once the meal is done, is the dustbin, so also, after spiritual awakening, jnana has just about as much use as a used leaf-plate.

It is important for an awakened person to move towards the goal with effort and faith; there is no need for excessive knowledge. Rather, knowledge sows the seeds of ego in the human mind, which is not only a hindrance to the sadhaka's progress, but can be the cause of regression or misfortune. It may confound a traveller walking on the right path; it may cause him to lose his way. Knowledge is a double-edged sword: on one hand it can cut away obstacles on one's path, on the other it can itself become opposed to progress along the path. The sadhaka walking on the synthetic spiritual path can often, due to excessive knowledge, become ensnared in the talons of analytical tendencies; he can become separated from the longing for the great, and instead become self-centered. Leaving aside the worship of Khuda [God], he can begin to worship khud [self]. There is considerable possibility of falling into such situations.

Baba fostered, encouraged and always supported this fundamental mantra: mokshakárana-sámagryám bhaktir eva gariiyasii ("Among the causes for liberation, devotion alone is greatest"). So that knowledge does not become an obstacle on the path, so that it may not foster ego or confusion in the sadhaka, it is important for one to calm the mind by regularly casting it into the fire of karma. The higher the level of jnana, the more intense the fire of karma should be. Only then will the gross, crude jnana melt and liquefy, effectively being converted into bhakti rasa, "the flow of devotion." Baba used to

Seminars, the Spread of Knowledge, and the Aim of Sadhana

say that the fire of karma should burn twice as brightly as that of one's store of knowledge. This is the rule. Otherwise, it is not easily possible to kindle devotion. As long as the ego does not flow out through the eyes after melting into tears, it is not possible to extinguish it. Rabindranath Tagore also offered the same prayer to the Lord: nata kare dáo, tomár caranadhulár tale, sakala ahankára he ámár ("Make all my ego bow in the dust at your feet").

Baba also used to say that the real knowledge is atma jnana, which can only be fostered by devotion. Other than atma jnana all other knowledge is meaningless. He used to make repeated mention of the following shloka:

átmajnánaḿ vidur jnánaḿ jnánány anyáni yáni tu,
táni jnánávabhásáni sárasya naeva bodhana't.

("Knowledge of the self alone is 'knowledge'; all other knowledges are mere semblances of knowledge.")

Seminars, the Spread of Knowledge, and the Aim of Sadhana

Once the shallowness of worldly knowledge is fully demonstrated, it is natural, in the company of sadhakas, to undermine its value. Therefore, the interest in jnana gradually diminished among the sadhakas of Ananda Marga.

In the realm of philosophy this was correct. It was also in terms of one's personal world of sadhana. However, in the realm of pracara it was not useful. Baba did not make Ananda Marga a "Sadhaka Sangha" but rather a "Pracaraka Sangha." In other words, having imbibed the necessary knowledge, and having become well-established in the proper philosophy, it was obligatory for every sadhaka to appropriately propagate the Marga in the public arena. It was extremely necessary to distribute this rare treasure that one had gathered among the people. This distribution was possible by two means: one, by one's own ideal behaviour, and two, via pracara.

For pracara, knowledge of philosophy is essential. Without irrefutable logic, without indisputable proofs, today's predominantly intellectual society would not be influenced or ever surrender merely through teachings. This is why Baba developed a strategy for augmenting the knowledge of philosophy among the pracarakas. He instituted the arrangement of seminars at various places: at the diocese level, at the district level, at the block level, at the pancayat level, and at the village level. That is, seminars were to be held from the topmost levels to the lowermost – a simple, unique solution to firmly establish sadhakas at every level in philosophy. In addition to this, during the seminars every sadhaka was given special duties depending on his samskaras and on his interests, and he was encouraged to take up pracara in various regions. Repeatedly Baba would urge, "Organize seminars! Organize seminars!"

Seminars, the Spread of Knowledge, and the Aim of Sadhana

A time came when Baba gave such pressure to participate in seminars that sadhakas who did not participate in seminars were excluded from the Guru's darshana. Moreover, He made the participation in seminars one of the sixteen points. The year 1960 was announced to be the Seminar Year. Throughout the year, the focus of the organization was on seminars. Seminars alone were given great importance. After that, every year, seminars began to be organized regularly. During the annual and half-yearly Dharma Sammelans, dates for the seminars in various regions would be announced; the names of the trainers and the organizers would also be announced. The seminars would begin at the diocese level, and then as far as possible would be organized at the lower levels. Baba himself would give the topics for the seminar, and Baba would also impart prior training to the trainers. After Mahaprayana, seminars have continued to be organized.

Another advantage of the seminar was that a sadhaka could gradually be directed away from crude thinking, away from parochialism and blind beliefs, and become acquainted with the true essence of dharma. The sadhak could gain freedom from religious malpractices such as the worship of gods and goddesses, the use of the sacred thread and the hair-lock as caste marks, ritual sacrifices, idol worship, the belief in heaven and hell and in ghosts, the practice of prayer and fasting for a month, as well as social malpractices such as discrimination based on caste, the practice of dowry, and the practice of propitiating the dead via the shraddha feast.

On the successful completion of the seminar, invariably the trainer would promptly have to send a seminar-report to Baba. Everyone was aware of Baba's strictness with regard to rules; in this area, He would never tolerate any mistake or limitation. Everything was to be completed in time, and in the proper manner.

Seminars, the Spread of Knowledge, and the Aim of Sadhana

In the Seminar Year of 1970, the first diocese seminar was organized in Tatanagar. The seminar was successfully conducted as per the proper procedure. The trainer Dada had been chosen to give training at another seminar as well. Having prepared the Tatanagar seminar report, he requested a responsible Margii brother, Kali Da, to himself go and present the report to Baba. Since Kali Da himself was rather busy, he asked his son Surya to swiftly go to Ranchi with

the report. It was a Sunday. Surya complained a bit at first, and then consented to leave for Ranchi in two days. "Why two days later?" said the father, "Why not today?" He did not give an appropriate reply, but refused to go before two days had gone by.

As per his wish, he reached Ranchi two days later. As per the norm, at the right time, Baba proceeded to the jagrti for darshana. Surya was also present there at the time. In His discourse that day, Baba explained in great detail about the eight siddhis. He said that the siddhis are eight in number: anima, laghima, mahima, iishitva, vashitva, prapti, prakamya and antaryamitva. These are also known as the eight aishvaryas.

After His lucid explanation, Baba then gave a demonstration on antaryamitva. Baba called a sadhaka close to Him, and instructed him to enter the mind of the boy Surya who had come from Tatanagar. At the time, Surya was sitting quietly in one corner of the room. Baba Himself said that his father had asked the boy to leave for Ranchi in order to carry out a responsibility for the Marga. But he refused to leave that day, and has come to Ranchi two days later. Having given this account, Baba then asked the sadhaka on whom He was performing the demonstration to enter into Surya's mind to find out why he refused to come to Ranchi two days earlier.

Seminars, the Spread of Knowledge, and the Aim of Sadhana

By Baba's grace and direction, the sadhaka explained that Surya does not perform sadhana regularly, and his behaviour too was not appropriate for sadhana. Because of this his mind, encompassed by meaningless thoughts, had begun to fall. In that fallen state of mind he did not wish to come before Baba. This is why he asked for two days leave; he did a lot of sadhana in these two days, kept his mind in Baba bhava continuously and repeated his ista mantra as much as he could. As a result, he began to experience liberation from the baser tendencies of his mind. Baba asked, "Surya! Tell me, is what he is saying correct?" Seeing that Baba was pleased, he took courage to reply in the affirmative.

After this, Baba asked the sadhaka to see what Surya's condition was like two to three days ago; he asked him to describe what he saw on Surya's mental plate. The sadhaka replied that his mental plate was dark like the color of clouds in the rainy month of Shravana. Then Baba asked, what is the color of his mental plate after having done intense sadhana for forty-eight hours? The sadhaka said that now his mental plate has become pure white. The dark clouds are scattering and sparkling whiteness is being reflected. Then Baba explained that by doing intense sadhana for two days alone, it is possible to completely transform human psychology, to entirely change the color of the mind.

After that, Baba spoke about anima siddhi. Baba explained that on achieving control over this siddhi the sadhaka is able to make his mind extremely subtle, and is then able to enter anywhere. Baba then instructed the sadhaka to enter into Surya's body with his mind to see the condition of his internal organs, to see whether his organs were in a healthy condition or not. After a while, the sadhaka said that all his organs except for his kidneys were healthy. Then Baba asked Surya whether he knew about his disease. He said, "Yes! A recent medical examination had shown that there was some problem with the kidneys." Baba advised him to see a good physician and get treatment. The disease was as yet in its very initial phase; he would be completely cured with proper treatment.

Seminars, the Spread of Knowledge, and the Aim of Sadhana

That day through the demonstration He conducted, Baba proved that the eight aishvaryas that are under the authority of iishvara, God, are possible for every sadhaka to attain by dint of effort and earnestness. As a result, the entire universe comes within the mental perception of such a sadhaka; all of creation becomes completely transparent to him.

imam vivasvate yogam proktaván aham avyayam,
vivasván manave práha manur ikshvákave 'braviit.
("I, Krishna, taught this eternal Yoga to Vivasván; Vivasván taught it Manu; and Manu taught it to Ikshváku.")

"Am I Some Hospital?"

In the fifties, as has been mentioned several times before, Baba had only a few select disciples, with all of whom He maintained a familial relationship. Every disciple considered Baba to be the head of their family, and would act upon His advice on every family issue, big or small. The sadhakas would unhesitatingly approach Baba for solutions to the smallest of problems, without a second thought. Acarya Viswanath Prasad Singh is a resident of Muzaffarpur, Bihar, and he is still amongst us. Even though he is nearly ninety years old, he still contributes, as far as it is possible, to the work of the Marga. Seeing him other Margii brothers are immensely inspired. He is the source of great inspiration for householders as well as wholetimers alike.

Viswanathaji received initiation from Baba directly in the year 1954. In other words, he is a sadhaka of the very early years. At the time the organization had not yet been founded. It is obvious that he was very close to Baba, and shared an extremely familial relationship with Him.

Once Viswanathaji's paternal cousin arranged his daughter's marriage with a suitable groom. It was decided to give Rs. 8000 in dowry. At the time, neither had the Marga's revolutionary marriage been instituted, nor was there any restriction laid on the practice of dowry. Before the auspicious date and time of the marriage was fixed, the birth-charts of the couple were compared. One so-called brahmin laid the seeds of doubt, saying that if the marriage took place, one of the partners would die. The family members became worried, and did not quite know what to do. Viswanathaji's mother's brother's wife asked him to take Baba's advice in the matter. With this explicit intention in mind, Viswanathaji immediately came to Jamalpur. When he did sashtanga pranam, Baba asked him whether he had come to Him with a pressing family problem. In those days, disciples did not have the hesitation that was natural to later disciples. There was no need to hedge around the issue; Viswanathaji swiftly said, "Yes, Baba! There is a problem related to a marriage."

Baba said, "Alright, tell me, what is the name of the girl?"

"Am I Some Hospital?"

Viswanathaji said, "Minni."

Baba said, "Oh, that's her pet-name, what's her real name?"

Viswanathaji said, "Baba, I don't remember her real name."

Then Baba said, "Her name is Kamini."

"Am I Some Hospital?"

Viswanathaji said, "Yes, Baba, Kamini is her name."

Then Baba asked, "Okay, tell me the boy's name."

Viswanathaji again displayed his ignorance. Baba said, "His name is Vasudeva Sharma. The one who compared their birth-charts is a fraud of a pandit. He does not know how to read a birth-chart at all. In order to gain some importance he has said some nonsense. After marriage the future of the bride and groom is completely free of disaster. Without any anxiety, go ahead and arrange this marriage; there is no cause for worry." Viswanathaji returned home, fearless, free from worry; he told his family members what Baba had said. Hearing this everyone became extremely joyful. The wedding was then arranged with great ostentation.

Baba would remove the diseases of many a disciple by the mere snap of His fingers. He would alleviate the most terrible diseases, or make them disappear altogether. Those for which no treatment was possible, in which cases physicians, hakims and Ayurvedic doctors had all given up in helplessness, through Baba's causeless grace such impossible-to-cure diseases would be removed. But Baba would usually grace only His devotees in this manner, devotees who had been initiated, and who were close to Him.

"Am I Some Hospital?"

A short while after Viswanathaji took initiation, the elder brother of his son-in-law was struck by tuberculosis. In those days, T.B. was considered to be an incurable disease. When there was no other way out, Viswanathaji's relatives said that since he often goes to see Baba, he should take this gentleman along with him on his next visit. Perhaps He will grace him. Eight to ten people prepared themselves to accompany him. Viswanathaji thought this would be a good excuse for all of them to be initiated. Acarya Pranay Kumar Chatterji initiated them all, one by one. Bringing this group along, Viswanathaji came to the jagrti. He did sastanga pranam before Baba.

Initially Baba was a bit upset. He then instructed them to bring the patient before Him having bathed him in forty buckets of water. Hearing this instruction, the patient became half-dead with fear. He said, "Baba, even without death approaching, I will die."

Reassuring him, Baba said, "There is no cause for fear; nothing will happen to you." When he was brought before Baba after being

bathed, Baba told them to take the patient for a walk to the tiger's grave and back.

"Am I Some Hospital?"

When the patient who was nearly on his deathbed heard this, he screamed, "No, Baba! This time I will certainly die! This is impossible for me to do!"

Baba said, "I assure you that nothing will happen." The order was an order after all; no one had the courage to go against Baba's instructions. When one is dying, what will one not do to escape death? But how amazing! On his way back, the patient developed so much strength and courage that he returned to the jagrti without anyone's support. All were embarrassed that they should have doubted Baba at all, and became joyful, overwhelmed by His grace. In this manner, the incurable disease of this patient was cured as though by magic. All of them returned home filled with joy.

Baba asked Viswanathaji to stay on. He said, "Viswanath, this time, I forgive you. But what you did was not proper. It is best that a human being suffers through the samskaras accumulated from his previous life. For how many people will I find cures going against the norms of prakrti? And will you propagate the Marga in this manner? If this is the case, then sick people will flock to Jamalpur. Tell me, won't this happen? Am I some hospital, that anyone who wishes can come to me, whenever they like, for treatment?"

tava tattvam na jánámi kiidrsho 'si maheshvara,

("O Maheshvara, I do not understand your essence; what is your nature?
Whatever your nature may be, to you I bow, O Mahadeva.")
