Yama and Niyama: an Inseparable Part of Sadhana

There are six accepted stages of self-realization in the realm of meditation: salokya, samipya, sayujya, sarupya, shashthi, and kaivalya. In this manner, there are four states in the world of meditation: yatamana, vyatireka, ekendriya, and vashikara. Establishment in these states and the savoring of these divine realizations are possible when a human being performs the sadhana of ashtanga yoga, with intentness and patience, with belief and faith. The eight aspects of sadhana are: yama, niyama, asana, pranayama, pratyahara, dharana, dhyana and samadhi. Samadhi is also divided into two states: savikalpa samadhi and nirvikalpa samadhi. In order to reach the highest levels of samadhi it is absolutely necessary to be completely established in yama and niyama. There are five yamas and five niyamas, making ten yama-niyamas in all. The five yamas are: ahimsa, satya, asteya, brahmacarya, and aparigraha. The five niyamas are: saoca, santosa, tapah, svadhyaya and isvara-pranidhana.

ahiḿsásatyásteyabrahmacaryáparigrahá yamáḥ;
shaucasantoṣatapassvádhyáyeshvarapraṇidhánáni niyamáḥ.

Without faithfully and sincerely following the ten yamas and niyamas, it is completely impossible for a human being to conquer the shadrpu (kama, krodha, lobha, moha, mada and matsarya), the ashtapasas (ghrna, shanka, bhaya, lajja, jugupsa, kula, shila and mana), and the shaddosas (nidra, tandra, bhaya, krodha, alasya and dirghasutrata). "Without yama and niyama, sadhana is an impossibility. Hence the Lord's command is also to follow yama and niyama." This is Shrii Shrii Anandamurtiji's Supreme Command, to follow which is the inalienable duty of every Ananda Margii.

Baba gave the utmost emphasis on strictly following yama-niyama. During His lifetime, from the beginning to the end, He stressed moral values based on yama-niyama. He gave new explanations for the traditional definitions of yama-niyama; his explanations were

Yama and Niyama: an Inseparable Part of Sadhana

practical and could be followed. The explanations of yama-niyama are not the subject of this chapter; those reminiscences are spoken and written of, here, which make amply clear, and indisputably prove the importance that Baba placed on yama-niyama.

Marga Guru Baba never made any compromises in His beliefs, or His pattern of work, due to any social, economic, political or other circumstantial pressures He may have faced. Whether it was the initial difficulties and problems that arose in pracara, or whether it was the later problems created by the government, He stood steadfastly by His principles. In His beliefs, in His style of work, nowhere, ever, did He make any compromise in the principles of yama and niyama.

From the outset, Baba had laid down a strict command: "Morality is the base, spirituality is the path, and divinity is the goal." Without a solid foundation, it is not possible to walk the path of spirituality and reach one's destination; this robust foundation is morality, morality alone. The fostering of this morality is possible via the strict adherence to yama-niyama alone; on this firm foundation alone can one build one's spiritual life. In other words, Baba established, propagated and represented yama-niyama as an intrinsic part of sadhana. Yama-niyama is the essential ingredient in the fire-sacrifice of sadhana; it is the obligatory oblation in the fire of sadhana. The strictness with which yama-niyama is followed nourishes and cleanses one's sadhana. There can be no complaint of the slightest hesitation or wavering in this conception.

There is no doubt that it is not easy to strictly follow yama-niyama in the unconducive environment of the corrupted psychology of the Kali Yuga. In today's polluted times, it is just as difficult to adhere to yama-niyama as it is to protect a newly-sprouted shoot from a herd of goats. However, the ability to strictly adhere to one's oaths in the face of difficulties is another name for sadhana. That which is easily possible cannot be the goal, and the effort to achieve it cannot be called sadhana. To walk this path, in those times and in these, is a difficult matter. After taking initiation many sadhakas cannot bear the high heat of yama-niyama, and even though they had initially associated themselves with the Marga, they leave the Marga, mid-way.

Yama and Niyama: an Inseparable Part of Sadhana

If a sadhaka tried to hide his weaknesses and mistakes, Baba would occasionally expose them in public. Unless one is strictly established in yama-niyama, it is not possible for sadhakas to stay in the Marga. At every step, Baba would proclaim the importance of yama-niyama in the life of a sadhaka – whether it was at the time of personal contact or general contact. Whenever Baba would perceive some moral flaw in a sadhaka, He would not refrain from displaying His annoyance. In truth, he wanted to cleanse the character of the sadhaka. Within His scrutiny was hidden His grace.

In the beginning, many well-established personalities of society became associated with the Marga with full force. Impressed by Baba's divine personality, His scientific philosophy and His easy meditational practices many well-reputed persons came to the Marga. Most of them were government officials. But in a governmental office environment where immorality and unethical behaviour was rampant, it was difficult for new sadhakas to adhere to the principles of yama and niyama. Despite great efforts, occasionally mistakes would be made. It was not easy to keep hidden the breaking of rules, because omniscient Baba would expose in a general darshana those respected and venerable officers and the immoral behaviour of non-governmental persons. He would reveal their punishable offences. Because of their egos being hurt, and their not being able to follow yama-niyama, they would often leave the practice of sadhana, and desert the Marga.

The householders; tattvikas and acaryas who were involved with propagating the Marga (there were no wholetime workers then) used to mentally complain about Baba's strictness. They would bring new sadhakas to the Marga with such effort, while Baba would banish them in a trice. They wished that Baba would be less strict with new sadhakas with regards to the practice of yama-niyama. Everyone felt this way, but no one had the courage to bring this up with Baba.

Yama and Niyama: an Inseparable Part of Sadhana

Acarya Viswanathaji of Muzaffarpur, Bihar, was a sufficiently senior sadhaka and acarya, and very close to Baba as well. (He is the same Acaryaji whose reminiscence has been previously presented in "Am I Some Hospital?") He eventually spoke out. He said to Baba, "Baba! We have to face many troubles in the work of pracara. There are many inconveniences involved."

Baba asked, "Tell me, what are your problems?"

Viswanathaji said, "There are two types of problems. First, You keep your magnetic personality under wraps; like a pearl in an oyster You keep Yourself hidden from the public. If You were to remove this covering veil from about You, then people could be easily awakened. Why don't You do so? You Yourself are a storehouse of unending knowledge, but You keep Yourself shut up in a room, while You send

us dim-witted ones for pracara. What capabilities do we have after all? Why don't You come out of Your disguise and reveal Yourself?"

Yama and Niyama: an Inseparable Part of Sadhana

Baba said, "Viswanath! What you say is correct, but don't you see how imbalanced and unnatural you people become when you come in contact with me? You begin to cry, to dance, to make the humkara sound, your kulakundalini is awakened. If every ordinary person, if the general public begins to get affected like this, it will be very difficult to control them, isn't it? No, no, it is better that you yourselves, having chosen those people who love God, bring them to Me." Saying so, Baba humoured him, and then, made him accept His views. Immediately deflecting the issue, Baba asked, "Okay, what is your second problem? Tell me!" Viswanathji said, "Baba! Your strictness with regards to the adherence to yama-niyama is, in fact, the biggest problem. Because of this huge obstacle, no one wishes to learn sadhana, and even if he takes diiksha, You expose his faults in public, and turn to naught the reverence in which he is held by the world. How will any well-respected person tolerate his own dishonouring? You tell us. If You were to relax the norms of yama-niyama just a little bit, if not for all time, at least in the beginning stages, it would make our pracara work much easier. When people join in large numbers then you can again become more demanding."

Baba then became solemn and said, "You see, Viswanath, my mission is a human-making mission. It is not a mission to gather disciples. I have resolved to create a healthy, beautiful society based on morality. It is not my aim to gather together a flock of sheep. Know this well!" Baba then went on to narrate a mysterious but true story. A certain Prime Minister of India – Baba named him, but for the sake of courtesy it is not appropriate to expose him here – was the greatest disciple and eulogisor of Gandhiji.

"He was a strong supporter and firm propagator of each of Gandhiji's teachings. Whatever Bapu would say, it would be as though written in stone for him. All Congress workers acknowledged the strength of the Prime Minister's ideals. Today that very Prime Minister unhesitatingly lives in five-star hotels, and eats meat and drinks alcohol. In public gatherings, he speaks of the welfare of the poor and downtrodden, but in private he lives a life of merriment. Do you wish that I should gather around me indisciplined immoral followers?"

Yama and Niyama: an Inseparable Part of Sadhana

Viswanathji had no response to give. Then, Baba touched his trikuti, with his big toe, and said, "Look, I have something to show you." After a while, he asked, "What do you see?"

| | | |---|---| | Viswanathji : | I don't see a thing. | | Baba : | Now look! | | Viswanathji : | Yes, Baba! I can see something now. It looks like a bazaar. There are shops on two sides of the road. It is very crowded. | | Baba : | Look further! | | Viswanathji : | Yes, Baba! I can now see a temple. | | Baba : | No, no! You're acquainted with this place. Think a little and recognize it. | | Viswanathji : | Yes, Baba! This is the Viswanatha Temple of Kashi. | | Baba : | What else do you see? | | Viswanathji : | Outside the temple, people are standing in a queue: the blind, the lame, the leprous. | | Baba : | Who are these people? | | Viswanathji : | Baba! They are a crowd of beggars. | | Baba : | Do you see a leprous man sitting in their midst? (Baba gives him a close-up view of this leprous man through his omniscient camera.) | | Viswanathji : | Yes, Baba! I can see clearly. | | Baba : | Now I give you the divine vision to see the past life of this leprous individual. Look into his past life! | | Viswanathji : | Yes, Baba! I see a white man wearing a white wig on his head. Perhaps he is a judge. | | Baba : | Yes, he is a judge. He is a British judge during the time before Independence. Now see his bad actions, and tell me what he is doing! | | Viswanathji : | Baba! I see that in his closed room he is pocketing some money. | | Baba : | Yes, in his private cabin he is taking a bribe from his lawyer. The general public did not know that he was the greatest of all those who accept bribes. He took undue advantage of his rank, gave false judgements on innocent convicts, put them through great misery, and today he is reaping the consequences of the bad deeds of his past life as a leper. |

After this, Baba asked Viswanathji to open his eyes and said, "Do you wish that we should give a place within the Marga to all such people who accept bribes? Should we gather together a crowd of immoralists? Do you now understand the importance of morality?"

Viswanathji immediately realized his mistake, and said, "Baba, I made a mistake for having even thought in this manner. In the future, I will never downplay the importance of yama-niyama."

Yama and Niyama: an Inseparable Part of Sadhana

Benevolent Baba at that moment, at that very place, graced Viswanathji with His blessings.

satyam eva jayate, nānṛtam
("Truth alone is victorious, not untruth.")

---

Porter Number One

When the Lord opens the sack of His limitless grace the recipient's basket is invariably too small. It has indeed been well-said that when the Giver wishes to give, He gives bounteously. That devotee is indeed fortunate who has been drenched from head to toe in the rain-showers of the Lord's divine grace. What amazing good fortune! It is absolutely impossible to understand the cause behind the Lord's grace. This is why His grace is always causeless. To grace is not the Lord's duty, rather, it is His nature. The showers of His grace rain down every hour, every minute, but we human beings are not drenched by them; we cannot bathe in the downpour of His grace, due to the umbrella of ego, or due to the curtain of His māyā's play.

The devotee is always drenched in devotion to the Lord; every moment he experiences the rain of grace. For him every situation is the Lord's gift of grace, because He can never be ungracious. From the mundane point of view, life situations can be pleasurable as well as painful, but the mark of a devotee is that he never thinks that His Lord is wrong. His every decision is benevolent; his every gift is beneficent. He can never think in terms of going against our welfare. The fact that we breathe in is due to His infinite grace; when we breathe out it is due to His unbounded grace. His grace-giving hand ever blesses us in varābhaya mudrā. There is nothing called incident or accident. Every happening is pre-determined and is designed for our welfare. We are a slightly worried and fearful of the darkness of the present because we are unacquainted with the approaching dawn. For the sake of the bright future of tomorrow, the Lord covers the present in darkness for a while. He has full knowledge of the past and the future; we are ignorant, innocent. The devotee always sustains himself by this healthy mentality.

An intellectual always attributes every situation to reactive momenta from one's past life: good reactions of good works, bad reactions of bad deeds. He says that this indeed is Prakrti's unchangeable steadfast law. The Lord does not ever interfere in this. All deeds are brought about by the reactions to deeds in this life or the previous life. In this, there is neither the Lord's particular grace,

Porter Number One

nor any particular lack of grace. Every reaction is in exact measure to the past deed: not a bit less, nor a bit more. This is the intellectual's logical mentality. Those karmis who have risen above these intellectual arguments into the arena of their experiences, those who have tasted the sweet juice of the Lord's liila and grace in the garden of their experiences—can anyone ever negate these? He who has lived his life as a part of the Lord's liila, he who has played the play of magic with the Magician's troupe, as his attendant—how can one discount his personal experiences? The Lord, the ocean of compassion, at the time of danger could become Draupadi's garment, and could naught the effect of the poison that Mira drank. He can control and destroy every affliction of his devotee in a trice. He is not subordinated to the rules of Parama Prakrti. He can even give His life for those He thinks are His. Becoming a servant, He can serve them.

Baba's endless storehouse of liila is filled with such countless reminiscences. This story is associated with the sannyasi life of Dada Nareshwarananda Avd. Born in Bengal, this worker became a sannyasi at the dawn of his life. Being overcome by Baba's sweet attraction, he had completely surrendered himself at His feet.

If in the Jamalpur phase householders could enjoy the treasure of Baba's endless love and His unparalleled grace, in the Parthasarathi phase, Baba's contact was mostly limited to wholetime workers. The Parthasarathi chapter was one of endlessly strict adherence to duty and the strict conduct of work. Baba would extract work from everybody, and was occupied night and day with the workers. Encouraging and directing them, moment by moment, would completely occupy His daily routine. In those days He was always engrossed in organizational work. He often did not have time to even converse with householders. Every moment He was surrounded by wholetime workers. He would punish them, He would scold them, but He also gave them His boundless love. Scolding and admonishment were His act, whereas love was the reality. Some Dadas would keep away from Him for fear of scolding and the anxiety of being publicly humiliated, while others remained extremely close to Him, fearlessly, unhesitatingly.

Porter Number One

Those workers who were simple, who spoke clearly and who were brave, who were fearless and unhesitant, but not brash or insolent, Baba gave special love. Baba's response to them was one of love—a love that was extremely sweet and intimate. (Anandanagar's Dadhici, Dada Amitananda, who is now no more, is remembered as one of those special, fearless, unhesitant workers. Towards them, Baba's limitless love will always remain something quite memorable.) Whichever Dada would work with all his mind and heart for the organization, Baba would be pleased with him. He would protect these surrendered workers in danger or in ordinary circumstances, directly or indirectly. If it was necessary He would even unhesitatingly do their work by becoming their servant.

Acarya Nareshwarananda Avadhuta, the central figure in this story, was also a special recipient of Baba's grace. He was young, raw, innocent, guileless, brave and ready to sacrifice his all—in other words, he had all the qualities that Baba loved. His courage and sacrifice were praiseworthy in themselves. He first decided to become a wholetime worker and then took initiation. No sooner did he take initiation than he was ready to sacrifice his life. Turant dana, mahakalyana ("Immediate charity leads to great beneficience"). Very swiftly, he began to move along the path of kalyana. Immediately after his personal contact, he left for a training camp in order to gain training. This is indeed an incomparable example of quick decision-making. He was initiated in the city of Burdwan, West Bengal, in the middle of the night at around 11 or 12 o'clock. He immediately left for Kolkata; at dawn he arrived there. Very soon he received Personal Contact with Baba, and immediately after, he left for training. At the time he was merely eighteen years of age; there was not a single adolescent hair on his face. This courageous and swift decision of his was something that Baba was aware of from a mundane point of view. After training, he worked in India for a short time; soon he was posted overseas. He was posted to Berlin Sector in a samaja-related post. Dada became very anxious on hearing of his posting.

Porter Number One

Not only was Dada Nareshwarananda himself anxious, but the other workers were also rather alarmed. How strange that an entirely inexperienced newly trained worker had been appointed to such a high and responsible post, and that too in an unknown new environment, in a new unfamiliar region, across the seven seas from India, to a highly intellectual place such as Europe! All the workers were extremely surprised by this posting. Dada neither knew how to speak English, nor was he very well-educated in a worldly sense. He himself was very bewildered and anxious about his new posting. He had never stepped out of Bengal in his entire life, and now suddenly was being sent outside India.

His mind and heart were beginning to panic. He made it clear that he would not accept this new posting, and planned to complain to

Baba about it. The senior Dadas told him not to; they tried to explain things to him, but he was not ready to accept their advice. During the general reporting, he began to question Baba about his new posting. A senior Dada scolded him, and asked him to be silent. Baba stopped him, saying, "Tell him to meet with me alone." After the general reporting of workers had ended, he was given permission to meet with Baba alone.

In his personal meeting Baba asked him what his complaint was. He said that he did not wish to go abroad. Baba said, "If you have a problem with some organizational decision it is better to speak about your difficulty to me in person, rather than in a general reporting session. It is not appropriate for you to break the discipline of the general meeting. I am not only your Baba, I am also the head of the organization. It is your duty to keep in mind the respect given to the Head as well as your boundaries." He then explained, "What is your destiny? What is your future? What do you want to do in the future? What do you wish to become? This, I know better than you. Why are you so anxious and concerned? I know well what work I should get done through whom, when I should get the work done, where it should be done. It is your duty to work; wherever you are sent you should continue to work. You have been appointed to a good post. Why do you have a problem with it? What is your concern?"

Porter Number One

Despite all sorts of encouragement and assurances given by Baba, his imbalanced mind did not come under control. He stubbornly continued to insist that he did not wish to go abroad. Baba asked, "Okay, tell me, Nareshwarananda! Why don't you wish to go?"

"There is not one but many problems; what can I say?" he began his complaints.

Baba said, "Okay! How many problems do you have? Take a pen and paper, and list all your problems, one by one." He put down on paper all the possible problems that were prancing about in his brain, and he prepared his mind to argue and debate with Baba.

"Okay!" said Baba, "now read them out one by one."

Porter Number One

The Avadhuta said, "The first problem is this that I do not know any other language other than Bengali. I don't even know how to speak English well."

Baba said, "You see, no one knows a language from birth; by constant listening, one learns. Depending on the environment in which one lives, one begins to learn the language of that area. And who told you that in Berlin sector people speak English? Apart from the little country of England, English is not much spoken anywhere else; with the amount of English you know, your work will surely be done."

Baba again said, "Now, tell me, what is your second problem?"

He said, "The second problem is that I know nothing of the social customs and traditions, and the local culture."

Porter Number One

Baba said, "The culture of the entire world is one. Yes, there is a difference in the local customs and traditions of every country; in the various states of India one actually notices the most regional differences. Wherever a person lives, he learns the customs of that area. I have full confidence that you will certainly learn these. This is not a huge problem." Then Baba said, "Now tell me, what is your third difficulty?"

He said, "You know that I am a new worker. Upto now I myself have not fully understood our philosophy. How will I explain it to others? How will I debate with others?"

Baba said, "Whatever you have learnt in training is sufficient. If you feel any lack, take a few books with you on social philosophy. As you continue to read, you will learn. This is not a problem at all. With regard to every issue, you are making a mountain of a molehill." After a while Baba said, "Now, tell me your fourth problem."

He said, "Baba I have not the least information on how I should work in those new countries, how I should do pracara there. What will I do once I get there?"

Porter Number One

Baba said, "You are not the first worker in this sector, nor the only worker. There are many senior dadas there. Karunanandaji is the Sectorial Secretary there; the head of your department is Krtashivanandaji, and there are many other senior Dadas. They are experienced; they will guide you well. They will easily solve this problem of yours; you do not have any cause for worry." After a while, Baba, the storehouse of compassion, said, "Okay, I will teach you a secret. Whenever you meet any unknown person, just smile, and your work will be done." He then said, "Okay, now tell me your fifth problem."

He said, "How will I do the work which I do not know how to do?"

Baba said, "As long as you do not learn, how will you do it? You see, if someone says, 'I will not go into the water because I do not know how to swim', how will he learn to swim? In order to learn to swim, one will have to enter the water. As long as he does not flap his hands and feet about in the water, he will never learn how to swim. This is the same principle that applies to pracara." Then Baba said, "Any other problem?"

He finally said, "No, Baba I don't have any other problem. I now have enough courage to go; now I will go."

Porter Number One

Baba was pleased with his decision, and said, "You see, all the problems that you listed on paper, were, in reality, not problems at all. All these problems are concoctions of your restless mind. You did not ask about the real problem. You should have said, 'I am going to a new place, and if I am trapped in a dangerous situation, what should I do?' So here, without your asking, I'm telling you: whenever you are in danger, and you see no option open to you, I will come to you in some form or other, in order to assist you. You have nothing to worry about. When I am the one who is sending you, the responsibility is mine. Isn't it so? Why are you unnecessarily becoming worried and fearful? Go, go on, free of care, and work with all your might. Success awaits you."

Feeling reassured, Dada Nareshwaranandaji began to engage himself in preparations to leave. By Baba's grace, his passport, ticket and other things were arranged swiftly and with ease. A well-off Margii of Mumbai, Iraniji, looked after him well, until he left him at the airport. In order to avail of the cheapest fare, Dada selected Syrian Airlines, which went to Copenhagen via Damascus. Up until here everything was working out just fine, but in Damascus, because there was no previous pracara, there was neither a Margii nor a wholetime worker. That unknown 'desolate' place was the beginning of his test of courage. The situation that arose can be encapsulated in the following saying: prathame gráse makshikapatah ("A fly falls in on the very first bite"). Though the Syrian Airlines ticket was inexpensive, along with it were associated numerous troublesome clauses. It was necessary to stay in Damascus, the capital of Syria, for four days, as the next flight to Copenhagen was only available four days hence.

Porter Number One

Being his first trip abroad and being unaware of the travel formalities and procedures, by the time he got out of the Damascus airport it was 1:00 a.m. The other problem was that he did not have sufficient warm clothing to protect himself from the cold. He had brought with him many books in order to study Ananda Marga philosophy. Since Baba had also instructed him to do so, he had brought with him the maximum number of books he could possibly carry. The weight of his books alone was 20 kilograms. This was the third problem. The fourth was that by the time he got out of the airport there was not a single passenger around; everyone had left for their respective destinations. Dada picked up courage to ask someone in his broken English about a hotel. The stranger did not understand a word; he moved away, shaking his head. He turned to another; he too went on without helping him. It was a desolate place; it was the first time he was travelling. Dada began to perspire. Where should he go? What should he do?

As the moments passed, Dada's anxieties burgeoned like the harvest from a fertile field; his heartbeats were becoming increasingly faster, just as the interest on debt increases by leaps and bounds. Internally, he grew angry with Baba. He said, "Where have you trapped me? Have false assurances ever solved any problems? You trapped me with your sweet words; making a total fool of me, you sent me here."

As he was complaining internally thus, suddenly a man came up to him. As soon as he came to Dada, he said, "Come on!" and he picked up his bag of books. Due to the tiring journey, Dada was in a state of bewilderment. He did not even speak with the stranger, and without giving it much thought, followed behind him. A little later he began to wonder whether the stranger was not going to take advantage of him. He began to walk a little faster. Finally this person, taking Dada along, climbed into a bus. Dada still did not muster the courage to ask him any questions. Due to his lack of confidence and his lack of knowledge of the language, he kept silent. After an hour's ride the bus stopped in front of a five-star hotel. The stranger booked a room for Dada in the hotel.

Porter Number One

To the very end, Dada did not pick up the courage to speak with him. It was a one-way conversation; only the stranger would occasionally speak. He explained to Dada that he had to stay in this hotel for four days, and then catch the flight to Copenhagen. He further explained to him in detail how he should find his way back to the airport on his return, what time to leave, and so on. Dada understood some things. The stranger also talked to the hotel staff for a long time. But what did he tell them? Dada did not understand, because he was speaking in the local language. Ultimately Dada found the courage to ask his well-wisher who he was, but he just smiled and left. By now Dada was completely exhausted from the back-breaking journey and all the related hassles of travel. He swiftly fell asleep.

After his early morning duties, his bathing and meditation, Dada realized that this was a five-star hotel. Did he have sufficient money to pay for a four-day stay in such a room? He went downstairs to the reception to make some inquiries. He was completely taken aback to find that the stranger had paid in advance for his four-day stay as well as the bus-fare for his return to the airport.

Dada felt reassured by the well-organized arrangements for his stay. He was certainly very comforted and felt greatly consoled. He then went outside the hotel to make some arrangements for his food and drink. He walked a short distance to find a market-place. In shop after shop, when he saw animal carcasses hanging, he began to feel nauseous. Because he had no previous experience, he thought that everyone in this country must be non-vegetarian. There must be nothing available to eat here other than non-vegetarian food. He returned to the hotel. The Margii brother Iraniji had given him some fruits for his travel. On these limited number of fruits Dada passed the remaining four days and four nights. With an empty stomach, he kept cursing Baba in dhyana.

Porter Number One

Finally Dada caught his next flight and reached Copenhagen. There the Marga workers greeted him at the airport. He did not face any problem at all in Copenhagen or in any other country. Gradually he learnt the customs of the country. He became familiar with the languages of the different countries. He became totally familiar with the new environment. He worked hard, and was successful. Almost a year later he returned to India. Now he was not the young ignorant sannyasi anymore. He was experienced, sufficiently self-assured and self-confident. Baba called him into his room. As soon as he entered; He asked, "So Nareshwarananda! Did you have any problems in your first travels?"

He immediately remembered the four days he had spent without food. He said, "Baba! Of course I had a lot of trouble, a lot of difficulty! I had to remain four days without food in the hotel!"

Baba said, "Who was responsible for your remaining hungry? Who is to blame? Me or you?"

Dada said, "You are to blame. It was You who threw me into difficulty."

Porter Number One

Baba said, "You see! A human being should also do his own bit. Can Paramatma every time, everywhere, keep feeding you with His own hands? The Lord has given you two hands and two feet. Isn't it the human being's responsibility to utilize these to their fullest? Has the Lord to do everything? If you had gone past the meat stores, you would have found fruit and dry-fruit stalls. But you did not walk ahead. And, tell me, who made your hotel arrangements? Who arranged the bus for you? You did not do a thing; everything was arranged by a stranger. That apart, who carried your bag for you like a porter?" Hearing this, Dada's eyes grew wide with wonder; tears began to stream from his eyes. Embarrassed, he fell down at Baba's feet. He remained there for a long while. He did not have the courage to meet Baba's eyes.

ananyásh cintnyanto mám ye janáh paryupásate,
teshám nityábhiyuktánám yogaksemam vahámyaham.
("I say that those ever-devoted people, and no others, who meditate upon me as they engage in worship, possess lasting attainment.")
